**Manila**,[^cite_note-13] officially the **City of Manila**,[^cite_note-14] is the [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_the_Philippines) and second-most populous city of the [Philippines](23440.md) after [Quezon City](284223.md). According to the 2024 census, it has a population of 1,902,590 people.[^cite_note-15] Located on the eastern shore of [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay) on the island of [Luzon](69375.md), it is classified as a [highly urbanized city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_the_Philippines#Independent_cities). With 44,935 inhabitants per square kilometer (116,380/sq mi), Manila is one of the world's [most densely populated cities proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population_density).[^cite_note-PSA20–NCR-8][^cite_note-PSApopden-9]

By 1258, a [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_people)-fortified [polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precolonial_barangay) called [Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_polity\)) existed on the site of modern Manila. On June 24, 1571, after the defeat of the polity's last indigenous ruler, [Rajah Sulayman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Sulayman), in the [Battle of Bangkusay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bangkusay), Spanish *[conquistador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador)* [Miguel López de Legazpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_L%C3%B3pez_de_Legazpi) began constructing the walled fortification of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) on the ruins of an older settlement from whose name the Spanish and English name *Manila* derives. Manila was used as the capital of the captaincy general of the [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies), which included the [Marianas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marianas), [Guam](11974.md), and other islands, and was controlled and administered for the Spanish crown by [Mexico City](18987.md) in the [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain). Manila is considered to be part of the world's original set of [global cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_cities) because its commercial networks were the first to extend across the Pacific Ocean and connect Asia with the [Spanish Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) through the [galleon trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_galleon). This marked the first time an uninterrupted chain of trade routes circling the planet had been established.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17]

Under the [American occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_occupation_of_the_Philippines), Manila became the first chartered city in the country, designated by Philippine Commission Act No. 183 on July 31, 1901.[^cite_note-18] It became autonomous with the passage of Republic Act No. 409, "The Revised Charter of the City of Manila", on June 18, 1949.[^cite_note-ra409-19][^cite_note-20]

In modern times, the name "Manila" is commonly used to refer to the entire metropolitan area, the [greater metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mega_Manila), and the [city proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_proper). [Metro Manila](72340.md), the officially defined metropolitan area, is the [capital region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_region) of the Philippines, and includes the much larger [Quezon City](284223.md) and the [Makati Central Business District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati_Central_Business_District).

The [Pasig River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasig_River) flows through the middle of Manila, dividing it into northern and southern sections. The city comprises [16 administrative districts](#Barangays_and_districts) and is divided into [six political districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_districts_of_Manila) for the purposes of representation in the [Congress of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_Philippines) and the election of city council members. In 2018, the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network) listed Manila as an "Alpha-" [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city),[^cite_note-21] and ranked it seventh in economic performance globally and second regionally,[^cite_note-22] while the [Global Financial Centres Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index) ranks Manila 79th in the world.[^cite_note-GFCI-23] Manila is also the world's second most natural disaster-exposed capital city after [Tokyo](30057.md).[^cite_note-Philippine_Daily_Inquirer-24] Furthermore, land subsidence and sea-level rise driven by climate change are challenges that the city will face in the future.[^cite_note-25]

## Etymology

See also: [Nicknames of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicknames_of_Manila)

The name Manila is derived from the [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_language) term Maynilà, which is commonly interpreted as either "where indigo plants are abundant" (may nilà) or "where nilad plants are abundant" (may nilad).

### May nilà

*Nilà* is derived from the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) word *nīla* ([नील](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E0%A4%A8%E0%A5%80%E0%A4%B2#Sanskrit)), which refers to [indigo dye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigo_dye) and, by extension, to [several plant species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigofera) from which this natural dye can be extracted.[^cite_note-baumgartner-26][^cite_note-chamberlain-27] The name *Maynilà* was probably bestowed because of the indigo-yielding plants that grew in the area surrounding the settlement rather than because it was known as a settlement that traded in indigo dye. Indigo dye extraction only became an important economic activity in the area in the 18th century, several hundred years after [Maynila settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Maynila) was founded and named.[^cite_note-baumgartner-26] *Maynilà* eventually underwent a process of [Hispanicization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanicization) and adopted the Spanish name *Manila*.[^cite_note-28]

### May nilad



Plate depicting the "nilad" plant (*[Scyphiphora hydrophylacea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scyphiphora_hydrophylacea)*), from Augustinian missionary Fray [Francisco Manuel Blanco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Manuel_Blanco)'s botanical reference, *Flora de Filipinas*

This etymology arose from the observation that, in [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language), *nilad* or *nilar* refers to a shrub-like tree (*[Scyphiphora hydrophyllacea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scyphiphora_hydrophyllacea)*; formerly *Ixora manila* Blanco) that grows in or near [mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove) swamps.[^cite_note-baumgartner-26][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-merrill-30] However, Baumgartner explained that it is unlikely that native Tagalog speakers would completely drop the final consonant /d/ in *nilad* to arrive at the present form *Maynilà*.[^cite_note-baumgartner-26] As an example, nearby [Bacoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacoor) retains the final consonant of the old Tagalog word *bakoód* ("elevated piece of land"), even in old Spanish renderings of the placename (e.g., *Vacol*, *Bacor*).[^cite_note-31] Linguist Vic Romero contends that it is actually not impossible for final consonant /d/ to shift into a glottal stop such as in *mapalad* to *pinagpalà* and *hangád* to *hangà.*[^cite_note-\:2-32]

The earliest known reference to this etymology was in the third volume of [John Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ray)'s *[Historia Plantarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_Plantarum_\(Ray_book\))* in 1704, originally lifted from the *Herbarium aliarumque Stirpium in Insula Luzone Philippinarum primaria nascentium...* by [Fr. Georg Josef Kamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Josef_Kamel)[^cite_note-Transactions_rolfe-33], and he mentioned that:&#x20;

*Nilad arbor mediocris, rarissimi recta, ligno folido, et compacto ut Molavin, ubi abundant Mangle, locum vocant Manglar, ita ubi nilad, Maynilad, unde corrupte Manila* (Nilad is an average tree, very rare straight, leafy wood, and compact like Molavin, where Mangle abounds, the place is called Manglar, so where nilad (abounds), Maynilad, whence the corruption Manila).

[^cite_note-Historia_Plantarum_bhl-34]

[^cite_note-\:2-32]

Examples of later adoption of this etymology include the name of a local utility company, [Maynilad Water Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynilad_Water_Services), alternative name of Manila City Hall, Maharnilad, and the name of underpass close to it, [Lagusnilad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagusnilad) (meaning "Nilad Pass").[^cite_note-35]

## History

Main article: [History of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Manila)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Manila).

### Early history



The [Laguna Copperplate Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription) is the oldest historical record in the Philippines. It has the first historical reference to [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)) and dates back to [Saka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaka_era) 822 (c. 900).

The earliest evidence of human life around present-day Manila is the nearby [Angono Petroglyphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angono_Petroglyphs), which are dated to around 3000 BC. [Negritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negritos), the aboriginal inhabitants of the Philippines, lived across the island of [Luzon](69375.md), where Manila is located, before [Malayo-Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian_languages) arrived and [assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) them.[^cite_note-36]

[Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_polity\)), along with [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)), were active trade partners with the [Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty) and [Yuan dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty) of China and flourished during the mid to later period of the [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty).[^cite_note-37] According to a Japanese encyclopedia [Wakan Sansai Zue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakan_Sansai_Zue), Luzon or Lusong (Maynila) was referred to as a "kingdom" south of [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-38]

During the 12th century, then-Hindu Brunei called "Pon-i", as reported in the Chinese annals *Nanhai zhi*, invaded Malilu 麻裏蘆 (claimed by various scholars to be the present-day Manila) as it also administered [Sarawak](28258.md) and [Sabah](28678.md), as well as the Philippine kingdoms of: [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan), [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu), [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) (Mindoro or Laguna), [Shahuchong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanmalan) 沙胡重 (present-day [Zamboanga](25167165.md)), Yachen 啞陳 ([Oton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oton)), and 文杜陵 Wenduling (present-day [Mindanao](20546.md), [Bintulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bintulu) or [Mindoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindoro)).[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] In the 13th century, Manila consisted of a fortified settlement and trading quarter on the shore of the Pasig River. Upon the conversion of [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei) from Hinduism to Islam, Manila also followed, as the Bruneian royal family also intermarried with Manila's royal family, as can be gleaned by the personage of [Rajah Matanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Matanda) who was simultaneously king of Manila while being a great-grandson of [Sultan Bolkiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Bolkiah) of Brunei.[^cite_note-JoaquinManila-41]

### Spanish colonial era

Main articles: [History of the Philippines (1565–1898)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1565%E2%80%931898\)), [Captaincy General of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_the_Philippines), and [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies)



Statue of [Rajah Sulayman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Sulayman) in Manila, Sulayman was the Crown Prince of the [Kingdom of Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Maynila) was the commander of the Tagalog forces in the battle of Manila of 1570 against Spanish forces.



1734 map of the [Walled City of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros). The city was planned according to the [Laws of the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_the_Indies).



[Ayuntamiento de Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayuntamiento_de_Manila) served as the City Hall during the Spanish Colonial Period.

On June 24, 1571, [conquistador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador) [Miguel López de Legazpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_L%C3%B3pez_de_Legazpi) arrived in Manila and declared it a territory of [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain), establishing a city council in what is now [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) district. Inspired by the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista), he took advantage of a territorial conflict between Hindu Tondo and Islamic Manila to justify expelling or converting Bruneian Muslim colonists who supported Manila while his Mexican grandson [Juan de Salcedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo) had a romantic relationship with [Kandarapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_Kandarapa), a princess of Tondo.[^cite_note-42] López de Legazpi had the local royalty executed or exiled after the failure of the [Conspiracy of the Maharlikas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_Conspiracy), a plot in which an alliance of [datus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datu), [rajahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raja), Japanese merchants, and the [Sultanate of Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire) would band together to execute the Spaniards, along with their [Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_Asian) recruits and Visayan allies.&#x20;

The victorious Spaniards made Manila the capital of the [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies) and of the Philippines, which their empire would control for the next three centuries. The city was founded by several European Spaniards and Mestizo Mexicans and even was garrisoned by 400 Native American [Tlaxcalans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tlaxcala_\(Nahua_state\)) who accompanied Salcedo from Cebu and were given pensions.[^cite_note-43] In 1574, Manila was [besieged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1574\)) by the Chinese pirate [Lim Hong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lim_Hong), who was thwarted by local inhabitants. Upon Spanish settlement, Manila was immediately made, by papal decree, [a suffragan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Manila) of the [Archdiocese of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Mexico). By royal decree of [Philip II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain), Manila was put under the spiritual patronage of Saint [Pudentiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pudentiana) and [Our Lady of Guidance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Guidance).[^cite_note-44] After the conquest of Manila from its Muslim aristocracy; the Spanish-Mexican conquistadors were awarded encomiendas with Esteban Rodríguez de Figueroa [holding an encomienda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Encomederos_and_Nobilities) near Manila; tribute figures not recorded.[^cite_note-45] The other one is Juan Maldonado, another Mexican an early encomendero; of whom limited data survives.[^cite_note-46] In the meantime, the Muslim aristocracy of Manila converted or fled, one such was Datuk Manila who fled to the [Sultanate of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Malacca) where he became a [Wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali) or Islamic Saint.[^cite_note-47]

Manila became famous for its role in the [Manila–Acapulco galleon trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_galleon), which lasted for more than two centuries and brought goods from Europe, Africa, and Hispanic America across the [Pacific Islands](341111.md) to Southeast Asia, and *vice versa*. [Silver](27119.md) that was mined in Mexico and Peru was exchanged for Chinese silk, Indian gems, and spices from Indonesia and Malaysia. Wine and olives grown in Europe and North Africa were shipped via Mexico to Manila.[^cite_note-Kane-48] [Crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World_crops) native to the [Americas](29833.md) like [chilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper), [papaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya), [sweet potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato), [chayote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chayote) *et cetera* were also introduced to Asia at this point from the Philippines, permanently shaping [Asian cuisines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_cuisine) since.[^cite_note-49] Because of the [Ming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming) ban on trade leveled against the [Ashikaga shogunate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashikaga_shogunate) in 1549, this resulted in the ban of all Japanese people from entering China and of Chinese ships from sailing to Japan. Manila became the only place where the Japanese and Chinese could openly trade.[^cite_note-50]

The [Manila Synod of 1582](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Synod_of_1582?action=edit\&redlink=1), the first Catholic [Synod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod) in the Philippines laid out the moral foundations for the incorporation of the archipelago into the Spanish Empire. Therein, the people had argued that native Filipinos already possess inherent dignity and sovereignty due to the precepts of [Natural Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_Law). And that any temporal power Spain has in incorporating the Philippines under the Spanish Empire is merely a servant to Spain's obligation or Supernatural Duty, mainly that is to save souls through faith in Christ; in which case they argued that it was just and moral to convert the Pagan (Hindu, Buddhist, and Animist) as well as Muslim; kingdoms of the Philippines into Christianity, in order to save their souls and that care should be taken to respect natives' rights and sovereignty, the best way of doing so, was to gently impose a more just and humane society under Christian Law which the Spanish did; by abolishing slavery, polygamy, polyandry, and infanticide, often practiced by the native Pagans and Muslims. Furthermore, Philippine society was materially enriched via trade with [Latin-America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin-America), and if any other power were to serve as obstacles for the increased progress and cultivation of the Philippines, the Spanish are morally bound to wage [Just War Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just_War_Theory) against them. As a result of pacification campaigns, religious conversions, colonizations, and settlement by the Spanish in cooperation with the local natives, the proceedings of the [Manila Synod of 1582](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Synod_of_1582?action=edit\&redlink=1) guaranteed native and Spanish rights and obligations amidst the Spanish-Mexican colonization of the Philippines and the later [1599 Philippines sovereignty referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1599_Philippines_sovereignty_referendums) were proclaimed, and therein, assent was given by the governed Filipino peoples, in accepting Spanish sovereignty.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52]

In 1606, upon the Spanish conquest of the [Sultanate of Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ternate), one of monopolizers of the growing of spice, the Spanish deported the ruler Sultan Said Din Burkat[^cite_note-53] of Ternate, along with his clan and his entourage to Manila, where they were initially enslaved and eventually converted to Christianity.[^cite_note-54] About 200 families of mixed Spanish-Mexican-Filipino and Moluccan-Indonesian-Portuguese descent from Ternate and Tidor followed him there at a later date.[^cite_note-55]

The city attained great wealth due to its location at the confluence of the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road), the [Spice Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_Route), and the [Silver Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_silver_trade_from_the_16th_to_18th_centuries).[^cite_note-56] Significant is the role of [Armenians](10918072.md), who acted as merchant intermediaries that made trade between Europe and Asia possible in this area. France was the first nation to try financing its Asian trade with a partnership in Manila through Armenian khojas. The largest trade volume was in iron, and 1,000 iron bars were traded in 1721.[^cite_note-57] In 1762, [the city was captured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1762\)) by [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) as part of the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War), in which Spain had recently become involved.[^cite_note-58] The [British occupied the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_occupation_of_Manila) for twenty months from 1762 to 1764 in their attempt to capture the [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies) but they were unable to extend their occupation past Manila proper.[^cite_note-Backhouse-59] Frustrated by their inability to take the rest of the archipelago, the British withdrew in accordance with the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)) signed in 1763, which brought an end to the war. An unknown number of [Indian soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_armies) known as [sepoys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepoy), who came with the British, deserted and settled in nearby [Cainta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cainta), Rizal.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61]



[Parián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pari%C3%A1n_\(Manila\)), or *Parián de Arroceros* was an area outside of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) built to house [Sangley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangley) ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Filipinos)) merchants during the [Spanish rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1565%E2%80%931898\)).

The Chinese minority were punished for supporting the British, and the fortress city Intramuros, which was initially populated by 1,200 pure Spanish families and garrisoned by 400 Spanish troops,[^cite_note-62] kept its cannons pointed at [Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo), the world's oldest [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown).[^cite_note-en.radio86.com-63] The population of native Spaniards was concentrated in the southern part of Manila and in 1787, La Pérouse recorded one regiment of 1,300 Mexicans garrisoned at Manila,[^cite_note-64] and they were also at [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite_City), where ships from Spain's American colonies docked at,[^cite_note-65] and at [Ermita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita), which was thus-named because of a Mexican hermit who lived there. The Hermit-Priest's name was Juan Fernandez de Leon who was a Hermit in Mexico before relocating to Manila.[^cite_note-66] Priests were not usually alone too since they often brought along [Lay Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lay_Brother) and Sisters. The years: 1603, 1636, 1644, 1654, 1655, 1670, and 1672; saw the deployment of 900, 446, 407, 821, 799, 708, and 667 Latin American soldiers from [Mexico at Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_settlement_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-Mexicans-67] The Philippines hosts the only Latin American established districts in Asia,[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] with the [Malate district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila) hosting [buildings mixing Mexican Baroque and Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate_Church) Muslim [Mudejar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudejar) styles.[^cite_note-Coseteng-70] The Spanish evacuated Ternate and settled [Papuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_people_of_New_Guinea) refugees in [Ternate, Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternate,_Cavite), which was named after their former homeland.[^cite_note-lipski-71] In 1603, Manila was also home to 25,000 Chinese[^cite_note-Estadismo1-72]<sup>: 260 </sup> and housed 14,437 native (Malay-Filipino) families, as well as 3,528 mixed Spanish-Filipino families.[^cite_note-Estadismo1-72]<sup>: 539 </sup>

The rise of Spanish Manila marked the first time all hemispheres and continents were interconnected in a worldwide trade network, making Manila, alongside [Mexico City](18987.md) and [Madrid](41188263.md), the world's [original set of global cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_silver_trade_from_the_16th_to_18th_centuries).[^cite_note-73] A Spanish Jesuit priest commented due to the confluence of many foreign languages in Manila, the confessional in Manila was "the most difficult in the world".[^cite_note-philislands-74][^cite_note-75] Juan de Cobo, another Spanish missionary of the 1600s, was so astonished by the commerce, cultural complexity, and ethnic diversity in Manila he wrote to his brethren in Mexico:

> The diversity here is immense such that I could go on forever trying to differentiate lands and peoples. There are Castilians from all provinces. There are Portuguese and Italians; Dutch, Greeks and Canary Islanders, and Mexican Indians. There are slaves from Africa brought by the Spaniards \[Through America], and others brought by the Portuguese \[Through India]. There is an African Moor with his turban here. There are Javanese from Java, Japanese and Bengalese from Bengal. Among all these people are the Chinese whose numbers here are untold and who outnumber everyone else. From China there are peoples so different from each other, and from provinces as distant, as Italy is from Spain. Finally, of the *mestizos*, the mixed-race people here, I cannot even write because in Manila there is no limit to combinations of peoples with peoples. This is in the city where all the buzz is. (Remesal, 1629: 680–1)[^cite_note-76]



[Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral) by [Fernando Brambila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Brambila), a member of the [Malaspina Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaspina_Expedition) during their stop in Manila in 1792.

After [Mexico gained independence from Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_War_of_Independence) in 1821, the Spanish crown began to directly govern Manila.[^cite_note-77] Under direct Spanish rule, banking, industry, and education flourished more than they had in the previous two centuries.[^cite_note-78] The opening of the [Suez Canal](29323.md) in 1869 facilitated direct trade and communications with Spain. The city's growing wealth and education attracted indigenous peoples, Negritos, Malays, Africans, Chinese, Indians, Arabs, Europeans, Latinos and Papuans from the surrounding provinces,[^cite_note-a112-79] and facilitated the rise of an *[ilustrado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilustrado)* class who espoused [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) ideas, which became the ideological foundations of the [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution), which sought independence from Spain. A revolt by [Andrés Novales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Novales) was inspired by the [Spanish American wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence) but the revolt itself was led by demoted Latin-American military officers stationed in the city from the newly independent nations of Mexico, Colombia, Venezuela, Peru, Chile, Argentina, and Costa Rica.[^cite_note-80] Following the [1872 Cavite mutiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1872_Cavite_mutiny) and the [Propaganda Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_Movement), the Philippine Revolution began; Manila was among the first eight provinces to rebel and their role was commemorated on the [Philippine Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Flag), on which Manila was represented by one of the eight rays of the symbolic sun.[^cite_note-81]

### American dominion era

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1898–1946)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1898%E2%80%931946\))

After the [1898 Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1898\)), Spain ceded the city to the United States. The [First Philippine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Philippine_Republic) based in nearby [Bulacan](252408.md) fought against the Americans for control of the city.[^cite_note-82] The Americans defeated the First Philippine Republic and captured its president [Emilio Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Aguinaldo), who pledged allegiance to the U.S. on April 1, 1901.[^cite_note-Magoc-2015-83] Upon drafting a new charter for Manila in June 1901, the U.S. officially recognized that the city of Manila consisted of Intramuros and the surrounding areas. The new charter proclaimed Manila was composed of eleven municipal districts: Binondo, [Ermita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita), Intramuros, [Malate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila), [Paco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paco,_Manila), [Pandacan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandacan), [Sampaloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampaloc,_Manila), [San Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel,_Manila), [Santa Ana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana,_Manila), [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Manila), and [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo,_Manila). The [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines) recognized five parishes as parts of Manila; Gagalangin, Trozo, Balic-Balic, [Santa Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa), and Singalong; and Balut and [San Andres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Andres,_Manila) were later added.[^cite_note-JN-84]



[Jones Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Bridge) in the 1930s

Under U.S. control, a new, civilian-oriented [Insular Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Government) headed by [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Philippines) [William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft) invited city planner [Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) (Founder of the "[City Beautiful Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_Movement)") to adapt Manila to modern needs.[^cite_note-85] The 1905 [Burnham Plan of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burnham_Plan_of_Manila) recommended improving the city's transit systems by creating diagonal arteries radiating from the new central civic district into areas at the outskirts of the city. It included the development of a road system, the use of waterways for transportation, and the beautification of Manila with waterfront improvements and construction of parks, parkways, and buildings.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87]

The planned buildings included a government center occupying all of Wallace Field, which extends from [Rizal Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Park) to the present [Taft Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft_Avenue). The Philippine capitol was to rise at the Taft Avenue end of the field, facing the sea. Along with buildings for government bureaus and departments, it would form a quadrangle with a central lagoon and a monument to [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal) at the other end of the field.[^cite_note-Torres-2010-88] Of Burnham's proposed government centers in [Luneta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luneta), only three units—the Legislative Building, and the buildings of the Finance and Agricultural Departments—were completed before [World War II](32927.md) began, with the war's onset, also destroying the former gracefulness that was the fruit of the City Beautiful Movement.

### Japanese occupation

Further information: [Battle of Manila (1945)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)) and [Manila Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Massacre)



A [TBF-1 Avenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TBF_Avenger) from [USS Essex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Essex) dropping a bomb over the Pasig River in Manila, targeting the dockyard, November 14, 1944



Manila destroyed during the [Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)) of the [Americans](19792942.md) and [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army) during [World War II](32927.md).

During the [Japanese occupation of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines), American soldiers were ordered to withdraw from Manila and all military installations were removed by December 24, 1941. Two days later, General [Douglas MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_MacArthur) declared Manila an [open city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_city) to prevent further death and destruction but Japanese warplanes continued bombing the city.[^cite_note-89] Japanese forces occupied Manila on January 2, 1942.[^cite_note-90]

From February 3 to March 3, 1945, Manila was the site of [one of the bloodiest battles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)) in the [Pacific theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War) of World War II. Under orders of Japanese Rear Admiral [Sanji Iwabuchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanji_Iwabuchi), retreating Japanese forces [killed about 100,000 Filipino civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_massacre) and perpetrated the mass rape of women in February.[^cite_note-Stich-2010-91][^cite_note-92] At the end of the war, Manila had suffered from heavy bombardment and became the second-most-destroyed city of World War II.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] Manila was recaptured by American and Philippine troops. The destruction the war wrought against Manila, made National Artist, Nick Joaquin Lament:

> "We are a city of layers. Every time we walk through the streets of Manila, we are treading upon the dust of several different cities that have flourished and perished on this same site. There was the Manila of the Rajahs, the Manila of the Conquistadors, the Manila of the Viceroys, and the Manila of the Commonwealth. Each was destroyed, and each was buried, and upon its grave the next one rose."

— Nicomedes Márquez Joaquín, *Manila: Sin City and Other Chronicles* (1977)

### Postwar and independence era

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1946–1965)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1946%E2%80%931965\))

After the war, reconstruction efforts started. Buildings like [Manila City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Hall), the Legislative Building (now the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Fine_Arts_\(Manila\))), and [Manila Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Central_Post_Office) were rebuilt, and roads and other infrastructures were repaired. In 1948, President [Elpidio Quirino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elpidio_Quirino) moved the seat of government of the Philippines to [Quezon City](284223.md), a new capital in the suburbs and fields northeast of Manila, which was created in 1939 during the administration of President [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon).[^cite_note-95] The move ended any implementation of the Burnham Plan's intent for the government center to be at Luneta. When [Arsenio Lacson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenio_Lacson) became the first elected [Mayor of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Manila) in 1952, before which all mayors were appointed, Manila underwent a "Golden Age",[^cite_note-RoseHistorianManila-96] regaining its pre-war moniker "Pearl of the Orient". After Lacson's term in the 1950s, Manila was led by [Antonio Villegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Villegas) for most of the 1960s. [Ramon Bagatsing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Bagatsing) was mayor from 1972 until the 1986 [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution).[^cite_note-97]

During the administration of [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos), Metro Manila was created as an integrated unit with the enactment of Presidential Decree No. 824 on November 7, 1975. The area encompassed four cities and thirteen adjoining towns as a separate regional unit of government.[^cite_note-98] On June 24, 1976, the 405th anniversary of the city's founding, President Marcos reinstated Manila as the capital of the Philippines for its historical significance as the seat of government since the Spanish Period.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100] At the same time, Marcos designated his wife [Imelda Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imelda_Marcos) as the first governor of Metro Manila. She started the rejuvenation of the city and re-branded Manila the "[City of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Man)".[^cite_note-101]

### Martial Law era

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1965–1986)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1965%E2%80%931986\))

Many of the key events of the historical period from the first major protests against the administration of [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) in January 1970 until his ouster in February 1986 took place within the city of Manila. The first, the January 26, 1970, State of the Nation Address Protest which kicked off the "[First Quarter Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Quarter_Storm)", took place at the Legislative Building (now the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Fine_Arts_\(Manila\))) on [Padre Burgos Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padre_Burgos_Avenue),[^cite_note-FreePressFeb71970-102] and the very last saw the [Marcos family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_family) flee [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) into exile in the United States.[^cite_note-TigerMoon-103][^cite_note-7DeadlyProtests-104][^cite_note-GazetteHistoryProtest-105]

The beginning weeks of Ferdinand Marcos' second term as president was marked by the [1969 balance of payments crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_balance_of_payments_crisis), which economists trace to his first term tactic of using foreign loans to fund massive government projects in an effort to curry votes.[^cite_note-Balbosas1992-106][^cite_note-Cororaton1997-107][^cite_note-CamilleDiola-108] In protest, protest groups led mostly by students decided to picket Marcos' 1970 State of the Nation Address at the legislative building on January 26. The protesters were initially bickering amongst themselves because both moderate reformist and radical activist groups were present and fighting to gain control of the stage. But all of them, regardless of advocacy, were violently dispersed by the [Philippine Constabulary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Constabulary).[^cite_note-Passionate-109][^cite_note-Daroy1988-110] This was followed by six more major protests which were violently dispersed, from the end of January until March 17, 1970.[^cite_note-7DeadlyProtests-104]

Instability continued the following year, with the most significant incident being the August 1971 [Plaza Miranda bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Miranda_bombing) caused nine deaths and injured 95 others, including many prominent Liberal Party politicians including incumbent Senators [Jovito Salonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jovito_Salonga), [Eddie Ilarde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Ilarde), [Eva Estrada-Kalaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eva_Estrada-Kalaw), and Liberal Party president [Gerardo Roxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerardo_Roxas), [Sergio Osmeña Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Osme%C3%B1a_Jr.), Manila 2nd District Councilor Ambrosio "King" Lorenzo Jr., and Congressman [Ramon Bagatsing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Bagatsing) who was the party's mayoral candidate for Manila.[^cite_note-Daroy1988-110]

Marcos reacted to the bombing by blaming the still nascent [Communist Party of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Philippines) and then suspending of the writ of Habeas Corpus. The suspension is noted for forcing many members of the moderate opposition, including figures like [Edgar Jopson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Jopson), to join the ranks of the radicals. In the aftermath of the bombing, Marcos lumped all of the opposition together and referred to them as communists, and many former moderates fled to the mountain encampments of the radical opposition to avoid being arrested by Marcos' forces. Those who became disenchanted with the excesses of the Marcos administration and wanted to join the opposition after 1971 often joined the ranks of the radicals, simply because they represented the only group vocally offering opposition to the Marcos government.[^cite_note-OscarFranklinTan20141208-111][^cite_note-Pimentel2006-112]

Marcos' declaration of [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_under_Ferdinand_Marcos) in September 1972 saw the immediate shutdown of all media not approved by Marcos, including Quezon City media outlets, including the Manila-based [Manila Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Times), [Philippines Free Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_Free_Press), The Manila Tribune and the [Philippines Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_Herald?action=edit\&redlink=1). At the same time, it saw the arrest of many students, journalists, academics, and politicians who were considered political threats to Marcos, many of them residents of the City of Manila. The first one was [Ninoy Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino) who was arrested just before midnight on September 22 while at a hotel on UN Avenue preparing for a senate committee session the following morning.[^cite_note-Daroy1988-110]

About 400 prominent critics of the Marcos administration were jailed in the first few hours of September 23 alone, and eventually about 70,000 individuals became [Political detainees under the Marcos dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_detainees_under_the_Marcos_dictatorship) - most of them arrested without warrants, which is why they were called detainees rather than prisoners.[^cite_note-binubura-113][^cite_note-ABS-CBN_News-114] At least 11,103 of them have since been officially recognized by the Philippine government as having been [extensively tortured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_torture_methods_used_by_the_Marcos_dictatorship) and [abused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_of_the_Marcos_dictatorship).[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-RocamoraBook-116] and in April 1973 [Pamantasan ng Lungsod ng Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamantasan_ng_Lungsod_ng_Maynila) student journalist [Liliosa Hilao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liliosa_Hilao) became the first of these detainees to be killed while in prison[^cite_note-Inquirer04172017-117] - one of 3,257 known [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing) during the last 14 years of Marcos' presidency.[^cite_note-RachelAGReyes201604122-118]

In 1975, Marcos formalized the creation of a region called Metropolitan Manila, incorporating the four cities of Manila, [Quezon City](284223.md), [Caloocan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caloocan), [Pasay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasay), and the thirteen municipalities of [Las Piñas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Pi%C3%B1as), [Makati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati), [Malabon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabon), [Mandaluyong](359686.md), [Marikina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marikina), [Muntinlupa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntinlupa), [Navotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navotas), [Parañaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%B1aque), [Pasig](359702.md), [Pateros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pateros), [San Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan,_Metro_Manila), [Taguig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taguig), and [Valenzuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valenzuela,_Philippines). And then he appointed his wife [Imelda Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imelda_Marcos), who had been angered by the revelation of his dalliances during the [Dovie Beams scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dovie_Beams_scandal), [Governor of Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Metro_Manila).[^cite_note-VirataBioBook-119]

Despite Marcos' declaration of martial law, poverty and other social issues persisted, so even with the military in his control, Marcos could not hold back the unrest. A major turning point was reached in Tondo in the form of the [1975 La Tondeña Distillery strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_La_Tonde%C3%B1a_Distillery_strike) which was one of the first major open acts of resistance against the Marcos dictatorship which paved the way for similar protest actions elsewhere in the country.[^cite_note-120] From then, Manila continued to be a center of resistance activity; youth and student demonstrators repeatedly clashed with the police and military.[^cite_note-121]

Another major protest was the [September 1984 Welcome Rotonda protest dispersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_1984_Welcome_Rotonda_protest_dispersal) at the border of Manila and Quezon City, which came in the wake of the [Aquino assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquino_assassination) the year before in 1983. International pressure had forced Marcos to give the press more freedom, so coverage exposed Filipinos to how opposition figures including 80-year-old former Senator [Lorenzo Tañada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorenzo_Ta%C3%B1ada) and 71-year-old Manila Times founder [Chino Roces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chino_Roces) were waterhosed despite their frailty and how student leader [Fidel Nemenzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Nemenzo) (later Chancellor of the University of the Philippines Diliman) was shot nearly to death.[^cite_note-MB-122][^cite_note-123][^cite_note-CesM16-124]

### The People Power Revolution

In late 1985, in the face of escalating public discontent and under pressure from foreign allies, Marcos called a [snap election](462915.md) with more than a year left in his term, selecting [Arturo Tolentino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Tolentino) as his running mate. The opposition to Marcos united behind Ninoy's widow [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino) and her running mate, [Salvador Laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Laurel).[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] The elections were held on February 7, 1986, an exercise marred by widespread reports of violence and tampering of election results.[^cite_note-NSM-127]

On February 16, 1986, Corazon Aquino held the "Tagumpay ng Bayan" (People's Victory) rally at [Luneta Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luneta_Park), announcing a civil disobedience campaign and calling for her supporters to boycott publications and companies which were associated with Marcos or any of his cronies.[^cite_note-128] The event was attended by a crowd of about two million people.[^cite_note-129] Aquino's camp began making preparations for more rallies, and Aquino herself went to [Cebu](160278.md) to rally more people to their cause.[^cite_note-130]

In the aftermath of the election and the revelations of irregularities, [Juan Ponce Enrile](3322486.md) and the [Reform the Armed Forces Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_the_Armed_Forces_Movement) (RAM) - a cabal of disgruntled officers of the [Armed Forces of the Philippines](23448.md) (AFP)[^cite_note-131] - set into motion [a coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_1986_Reform_the_Armed_Forces_Movement_coup) against Ferdinand and Imelda Marcos.[^cite_note-132] Enrile and RAM's coup was quickly uncovered, which prompted Enrile to ask for the support of Philippine Constabulary chief [Fidel Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Ramos). Ramos agreed to join Enrile but even so, their combined forces were trapped in [Camp Crame](1697214.md) and [Camp Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Aguinaldo), and were about to be overrun by Marcos loyalist forces.[^cite_note-peoplepower_eyewitness-133][^cite_note-RafaelWhatWasEDSA-134][^cite_note-Aussie-135]

Discovering what was happening, the forces which had been organizing Aquino's civil disobedience campaign went to the stretch of [Epifanio De Los Santos Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EDSA) (EDSA) between the two camps, beginning to form a human barricade to keep Marcos loyalist forces from attacking. The crowd grew even larger when Ramos telephoned Manila Cardinal [Jaime Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Sin) for help, and Sin went on Radyo Veritas to invite Catholics to join in protecting Enrile and Ramos.[^cite_note-McCargo-136] Seeing what was happening, multiple units of the Armed Forces of the Philippines defected from Marcos, with air units under the command of General Antonio Sotelo and Colonel Charles Hotchkiss, even performed calculated operations which included strafing the grounds of Malacañang palace with bullets and disabling gunships at nearby [Villamor Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villamor_Air_Base).[^cite_note-peoplepower_eyewitness-133]

The [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_administration) eventually decided to offer Marcos a chance to flee into exile. Shortly after midnight on February 26, 1986, the Marcos Family fled Malacañang and were taken to [Clark Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Air_Base), after which they went into exile in [Honolulu](13887.md) along with some select followers including [Fabian Ver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabian_Ver) and [Danding Cojuangco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danding_Cojuangco).[^cite_note-TigerMoon-103] Because the victory had been won by the civilians on the streets rather than the military, the event was dubbed the [People Power revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_revolution). Ferdinand Marcos' 21 years as President - and his 14 years as authoritarian leader - of the Philippines was over.[^cite_note-TigerMoon-103][^cite_note-RafaelWhatWasEDSA-134] After Manila has once again experienced [a battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(disambiguation\)) and surmounted another crisis, Nick Joaquin, famous National Artist for Literature then wrote:

> "It almost seems as if every problem, every crisis, arises just to prove the aliveness of this city; continually destroyed and continually rebuilt, ever decaying and ever re-greening, Manila has survived for four hundred years and will surely survive for four hundred more. Because it is a city of our affections..."

— Nicomedes Márquez Joaquín, *Manila, My Manila: A History for the Young* (1988)

### Contemporary

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1986–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1986%E2%80%93present\))



The [Binondo–Intramuros Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo%E2%80%93Intramuros_Bridge), opened in 2022, connecting the districts of [Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo) and [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros).

From 1986 to 1992, [Mel Lopez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mel_Lopez) was mayor of Manila, first due to presidential designation, before being elected in 1988.[^cite_note-137] In 1992, [Alfredo Lim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Lim) was elected mayor, the first [Chinese-Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Filipino) to hold the office. He was known for his anti-crime crusades. Lim was succeeded by [Lito Atienza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lito_Atienza), who served as his vice mayor, and was known for his campaign and slogan "Buhayin ang Maynila" (Revive Manila), which saw the establishment of several parks, and the repair and rehabilitation of the city's deteriorating facilities. He was the city's mayor for nine years before being termed out of office. Lim once again ran for mayor and defeated Atienza's son Ali in the 2007 city election, and immediately reversed all of Atienza's projects,[^cite_note-138] which he said made little contribution to the improvements of the city. The relationship of both parties turned bitter, with them both contesting the [2010 city elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_local_elections,_2010), which Lim won. Lim was sued by councilor Dennis Alcoreza on 2008 over [human rights](13831.md),[^cite_note-139] he was charged with [graft](2717335.md) over the rehabilitation of public schools.[^cite_note-140]

In 2012, [DMCI Homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DMCI_Homes) began constructing [Torre de Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_Manila), which became controversial for [ruining the sight line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_pollution) of Rizal Park.[^cite_note-141] The tower became known as "Terror de Manila" and the "national photobomber",[^cite_note-142] and [became a sensationalized heritage issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_de_Manila_controversy). In 2017, the [National Historical Commission of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historical_Commission_of_the_Philippines) erected a "[comfort woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_woman)" [statue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipina_Comfort_Women) on [Roxas Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxas_Boulevard), causing Japan to express regret about the statue's erection despite the healthy relationship between Japan and the Philippines.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]



[Santa Cruz district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Manila)

In the [2013 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Manila_local_elections), former President [Joseph Estrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Estrada) succeeded Lim as the city's mayor. During his term, Estrada allegedly paid [₱](227672.md)5 billion in city debts and increased the city's revenues. In 2015, in line with President [Noynoy Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noynoy_Aquino)'s administration progress, the city became the most-competitive city in the Philippines. In the [2016 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Manila_local_elections), Estrada narrowly won over Lim.[^cite_note-145] Throughout Estrada's term, numerous Filipino heritage sites were demolished, gutted, or approved for demolition; these include the post-war Santa Cruz Building, [Capitol Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Theater_\(Manila\)), El Hogar, Magnolia Ice Cream Plant, and [Rizal Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Stadium).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] Some of these sites were saved after the intervention of governmental cultural agencies and heritage advocate groups.[^cite_note-149] In May 2019, Estrada said Manila was debt-free;[^cite_note-150] two months later, however, the Commission on Audit said Manila was ₱4.4 billion in debt.[^cite_note-151]

Estrada, who was seeking for re-election for his third and final term, lost to [Isko Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isko_Moreno) in the [2019 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Manila_local_elections).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] Moreno has served as the vice mayor under both Lim and Estrada. Estrada's defeat was seen as the end of their reign as a political clan, whose other family members run for national and local positions.[^cite_note-154] After assuming office, Moreno initiated a city-wide cleanup of illegal vendors, signed an executive order promoting open governance, and vowed to stop bribery and corruption in the city.[^cite_note-155] Under his administration, several ordinances were signed, giving additional perks and privileges to Manila's elderly people,[^cite_note-156] and monthly allowances for Grade 12 Manileño students in all public schools in the city, including students of [Universidad de Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_de_Manila) and the [University of the City of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_City_of_Manila).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158]

In 2022, *[Time Out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Out_\(magazine\))* ranked Manila in 34th position in its list of the 53 best cities in the world, citing it as "an underrated hub for art and culture, with unique customs and cuisine to boot". Manila was also voted the third-most-resilient and least-rude city for the year's index.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] In 2023, the search site Crossword Solm utilizing internet geotagging, showed that Manila is the world's most loving capital city.[^cite_note-MostLoving-161]



View of Manila along [Roxas Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxas_Boulevard) in 2023

In August 2023, President [Bongbong Marcos](572460.md) suspended all [reclamation projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reclamation_in_Metro_Manila) in [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay), including those in the City of Manila.[^cite_note-162] However, the city has no objections and is willing to pursue the suspended reclamation projects.[^cite_note-163]

In 2024, Manila, as the nation's seat of government, witnessed the launch of the Fourth Philippine Human Rights Plan, aimed at advancing social justice, inclusivity, and human rights protection in line with international standards.[^cite_note-\:0-164]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Manila)



[Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay) sunset



[Manila Dolomite Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Dolomite_Beach) during the International Coastal Cleanup Day in September 2020



A map showing the territorial extent and assets or properties of Manila, including its territorial exclave [Manila South Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_South_Cemetery), and Manila Boystown Complex, which is a property in [Marikina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marikina) owned by the Manila city government.

The City of Manila is situated on the eastern shore of [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay), on the western coast of [Luzon](69375.md), 1,300 km (810 mi) from mainland Asia.[^cite_note-165] The protected harbor on which Manila lies is regarded as the finest in Asia.[^cite_note-166] The [Pasig River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasig_River) flows through the middle of city, dividing it into north and south.[^cite_note-arearef1-167][^cite_note-arearef2-168] The overall [grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grading_\(engineering\)) of the city's central, built-up areas is relatively consistent with the natural flatness of the natural geography, generally exhibiting only slight differentiation.[^cite_note-169]

Almost all of Manila sits on top prehistoric [alluvial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alluvium) deposits built by the waters of the Pasig River and on land reclaimed from [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay). Manila's land has been substantially altered by human intervention; there has been considerable land reclamation along the waterfronts since the early-to-mid twentieth century. Some of the city's natural variations in topography have been leveled. As of 2013, Manila had a total area of 42.88 square kilometers (16.56 sq mi).[^cite_note-arearef1-167][^cite_note-arearef2-168]

In 2017, the City Government approved five reclamation projects; the New Manila Bay–City of Pearl (New Manila Bay International Community) (407.43 hectares (1,006.8 acres)), Solar City (148 hectares (370 acres)), Manila Harbour Center expansion (50 hectares (120 acres)), Manila Waterfront City (318 hectares (790 acres)),[^cite_note-170] and [Horizon Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizon_Manila) (419 hectares (1,040 acres)). Of the five planned projects, only Horizon Manila was approved by the [Philippine Reclamation Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reclamation_in_Metro_Manila#History) in December 2019 and was scheduled for construction in 2021.[^cite_note-171]

Another reclamation project is possible and when built, it will include in-city housing relocation projects.[^cite_note-172] Environmental activists and the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines) have criticized the land reclamation projects, saying they are not sustainable and would put communities at risk of flooding.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] In line of the upcoming reclamation projects, the Philippines and the Netherlands agreed to a cooperation on the ₱250 million Manila Bay Sustainable Development Master Plan to oversee future decisions on projects on Manila Bay.[^cite_note-175]

### Barangays and districts



Manila is divided into six congressional districts.



A district map of Manila showing its sixteen districts

Manila is made up of 897 [barangays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barangay),[^cite_note-MNLLN-176] which are grouped into 100 zones for statistical convenience. Manila has the most barangays of any metropolis in the Philippines.[^cite_note-177] Due to a failure to hold a plebiscite, attempts at reducing its number have not succeeded despite local legislation—Ordinance 7907, passed on April 23, 1996—reducing the number from 896 to 150 by merging existing barangays.[^cite_note-178]

- **[District I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_1st_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 441,282)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers the western part of Tondo and is made up of 136 barangays. It is the most-densely populated congressional district and is also known as **Tondo I**. The district includes one of the biggest urban-poor communities; [Smokey Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokey_Mountain) on Balut Island was once known as the country's largest landfill where thousands of impoverished people lived in slums. After the closure of the landfill in 1995, mid-rise housing was built on the site. This district also contains the Manila North Harbor Center, Manila North Harbor, and Manila International Container Terminal of the [Port of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manila). The 1st District also covers Manila's borders with [Navotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navotas) and a part of the southern enclave of [Caloocan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caloocan).

- **[District II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_2nd_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 212,938)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers the eastern part of Tondo and contains 122 barangays. It is also referred to as **Tondo II**. It includes Gagalangin, a prominent place in Tondo, and [Divisoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisoria), a popular shopping area and the site of the [Main Terminal Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_\(PNR_station\)) of the [Philippine National Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Railways). The 2nd District also covers the rest of Manila's border with [Caloocan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caloocan).

- **[District III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_3rd_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 220,029)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers Binondo, Quiapo, San Nicolas and Santa Cruz. It contains 123 barangays and includes "Downtown Manila", the historic business district of the city, and the oldest Chinatown in the world. The 3rd District also covers a part of Manila's border with [Quezon City](284223.md).

- **[District IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_4th_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 277,013)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers Sampaloc and some parts of Santa Mesa. It contains 192 barangays and has numerous colleges and universities, which are located along the city's "[University Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Belt)", a *de facto* sub-district. Included here is the [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas), the oldest-existing university in Asia, which was established in 1611. The institution was home to at least 30 Catholic saints.

  [^cite_note-UnendingGrace-180]

  [^cite_note-TopUnis-181]

  &#x20;The 4th District also covers portions of Manila's borders with Quezon City and [San Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan,_Metro_Manila).

- **[District V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_5th_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 395,065)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers Ermita, Malate, Port Area, Intramuros, San Andres Bukid, and a portion of Paco. It is made up of 184 barangays and includes [Manila City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Hall), [Rizal Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Park), the historic Walled City, along with [Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral) and [San Agustin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church_\(Manila\)), a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md). The 5th District also covers portions of Manila's borders with [Makati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati) and [Pasay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasay). This district also includes the [Manila South Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_South_Cemetery), an [exclave](4848945.md) surrounded by Makati City.

- **[District VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila's_6th_congressional_district)** (2020 population: 300,186)

  [^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Legislative-179]

  &#x20;covers Pandacan, San Miguel, Santa Ana, Santa Mesa, and the rest of Paco. It contains 139 barangays and includes [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace), the residence and workplace of the [President of the Philippines](271126.md). Santa Ana district is known for its 18th century [Santa Ana Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana_Church) and [historic ancestral houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_houses_in_Santa_Ana,_Manila). The 6th District also covers the rest of Manila's borders with Quezon City, San Juan, Makati and Pasay.

### Climate



Manila's annual temperature and rainfall

Under the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) system, Manila has a [tropical monsoon climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_monsoon_climate) (Köppen *Am*), closely bordering on a [tropical savanna climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_savanna_climate) (Köppen *Aw*). Together with the rest of the Philippines, Manila lies entirely within the tropics. Its proximity to the equator means temperatures are high year-round especially during the daytime, rarely going below 19 °C (66.2 °F) or above 38 °C (100.4 °F). Temperature extremes have ranged from 14.5 °C (58.1 °F) on January 11, 1914,[^cite_note-183] to 38.6 °C (101.5 °F) on May 7, 1915.[^cite_note-184]

Humidity levels are usually very high all year round, making the air feel hotter than its actual temperature. Manila has a distinct [dry season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_season) lasting from late December to early April. A relatively lengthy [wet season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_season) that covers the remaining period, with slightly cooler daytime temperatures and slightly warmer nighttime temperatures. In the wet season, rain rarely falls all day, but rainfall is very heavy for short periods. [Typhoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon) usually occur from June to September.[^cite_note-185]
