### Natural hazards

See also: [List of earthquakes in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_the_Philippines)

[Swiss Re](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Re) ranked Manila as the second-riskiest capital city to live in, citing its exposure to natural hazards such as earthquakes, tsunamis, typhoons, floods, and landslides.[^cite_note-Philippine_Daily_Inquirer-24] The seismically active [Marikina Valley Fault System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marikina_Valley_Fault_System) poses a threat of a large-scale earthquake with an estimated [magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moment_magnitude_scale) of between 6 and 7, and as high as 7.6[^cite_note-MVFSUSGS-189] to Metro Manila and nearby provinces.[^cite_note-190] Manila has experienced several deadly earthquakes, notably those of [1645](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1645_Luzon_earthquake) and 1677, which destroyed the stone-and-brick medieval city.[^cite_note-Earthquake-191] Architects during the Spanish colonial period used the [Earthquake Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_Baroque) style to adapt to the region's frequent earthquakes.[^cite_note-City-192] The [Taal Volcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taal_Volcano) located to the south of Manila also poses a threat when it erupts.[^cite_note-193]

Manila experiences between five and seven typhoons each year.[^cite_note-AOML_FAQ_B2-194] In 2009, [Typhoon Ketsana (Ondoy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Ketsana) struck the Philippines, leading to one of the worst floods in Metro Manila and several provinces in Luzon with an estimated damages worth ₱11 billion ([US\$](18717338.md)237 million),[^cite_note-Ondoy-195][^cite_note-196] and caused 448 deaths in Metro Manila alone. Following the aftermath of Typhoon Ketsana, the city began to dredge its rivers and improve its drainage network.

### Parks and green spaces

See also: [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay) and [List of parks in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parks_in_Metro_Manila)



The [Arroceros Forest Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arroceros_Forest_Park) is considered as the "last lung of Manila".

[^cite_note-Inquirer-197]

[Metro Manila](72340.md) is situated in a variety of ecosystems including [upland forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upland_forest), [mangrove forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove_forest), [mudflats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudflat), sandy beaches, [sea grass meadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seagrass_meadow) and [coral reefs](87410.md). Metro Manila is home to urban parks, nature parks, plazas, nature reserves, and an arboretum. However, according to the Asian Green City Index, in 2007 Manila contained only an average of 4.5 square meters (48 sq ft) of green space per person, well below the index average of 39 square meters (420 sq ft)[^cite_note-198] and below the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) recommended minimum of 9 square meters (97 sq ft) per person.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]

The [Arroceros Forest Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arroceros_Forest_Park) is a 2.2-hectare (5.4-acre) [nature park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_park) situated in the heart of downtown Manila along the south bank of the [Pasig River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasig_River). Considered as the "last lung of Manila", the park was professionally planned in 1993 with its [secondary growth forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_growth_forest) of 61 different native tree varieties and 8,000 ornamental plants providing a habitat for about 10 different bird species.[^cite_note-phil-201]

### Pollution



[Smog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smog) in the [Quiapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo,_Manila)-[Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo) area

[Air pollution](10934212.md) in Manila is due to industrial waste and automobiles.[^cite_note-UNcyberbus-202][^cite_note-203] Swiss firm [IQAir](31884673.md) reported in December 2020 Manila experienced an average [PM2.5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PM2.5) concentration of 6.1×10<sup>−6</sup> g/m<sup>3</sup> (1.03×10<sup>−8</sup> lb/cu yd), which is classed as "Good" according to recommendations made by the World Health Organization.[^cite_note-204]

According to a report in 2003, the Pasig River is one of the most-polluted rivers in the world in which 150 metric tons (150 long tons; 170 short tons) of domestic waste and 75 metric tons (74 long tons; 83 short tons) of industrial waste are dumped daily.[^cite_note-205] The city is the second-biggest waste producing metropolis in the country with 1,151.79 tons (7,500.07 cubic meters (264,862 ft<sup>3</sup>)) per day, after Quezon City, which produces 1,386.84 tons (12,730.59 cubic meters (449,577 ft<sup>3</sup>)) per day. Both cities were cited as having poor management in garbage collection and disposal.[^cite_note-206] A 2021 report by Oxford University's Our World in Data estimated eighty one percent of global ocean plastic comes from rivers in Asia and the Philippines itself contributes one third of that number, and the Pasig River is one of the main contributors.[^cite_note-207]

Rehabilitation efforts have resulted in the creation of parks along the riverside and stricter pollution controls.[^cite_note-pianews-208][^cite_note-209] In 2019, the [Department of Environment and Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Environment_and_Natural_Resources) launched a rehabilitation program for Manila Bay that will be administered by different government agencies.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211]

## Cityscape



The [Roxas Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxas_Boulevard) skyline at night along [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay).

Manila is a [planned city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_town). In 1905, American architect and urban planner [Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) was commissioned to design the new capital.[^cite_note-212] His [design for the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burnham_Plan_of_Manila) was based on the [City Beautiful movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_movement), which favored broad streets and avenues radiating out from rectangles. Manila is made up of fourteen city districts, according to Republic Act No. 409—the Revised Charter of the City of Manila—the basis of which officially sets the present-day boundary of the city.[^cite_note-ra409-19] The districts [Santa Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa), which was partitioned from Sampaloc,[^cite_note-213] and [San Andres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Andres,_Manila), which was partitioned off from Santa Ana, were later created.

Manila's mix of [architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_style) reflects its, and the Philippines', turbulent history. During World War II, Manila was [razed to the ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)) by Japanese forces and the shelling of American forces.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215] After the war ended, rebuilding began and most of the historical buildings were reconstructed. Many of the historic churches and buildings in Intramuros, Manila's historic core, however, had been damaged beyond repair.[^cite_note-Wernstedt-1967-216] Manila's current urban landscape is one of modern and contemporary architecture. Manila's historic sites under the entry of *The Walled City and Historic Monuments of Manila* is currently being proposed to the tentative list for future [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md) inscription.[^cite_note-217]

### Architecture



The façade of the [Manila Metropolitan Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Metropolitan_Theater), designed by Filipino architect [Juan M. Arellano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_M._Arellano)



[Jones Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Bridge) was redeveloped in 2019 to "restore" it to its near-original design using [Beaux-Arts architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture).

Manila is known for its eclectic mix of architecture that includes a wide range of styles spanning the city's historical and cultural periods. Its architectural styles reflect American, Spanish, Chinese, and Malay influences.[^cite_note-218] Prominent Filipino architects including Antonio Toledo,[^cite_note-219] Felipe Roxas,[^cite_note-220] [Juan M. Arellano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_M._Arellano)[^cite_note-221] and [Tomás Mapúa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Map%C3%BAa) have designed significant buildings in Manila such as churches, government offices, theaters, mansions, schools, and universities.[^cite_note-222]

Manila is known for [its Art Deco theaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco_theaters_of_Manila), some of which were designed by [Juan Nakpil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Nakpil) and [Pablo Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Antonio).[^cite_note-223] The historic [Escolta Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escolta_Street) in Binondo has many buildings of [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) and [Beaux-Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture) architectural styles, many of which were designed by prominent Filipino architects during the American colonial period between the 1920s and the late 1930s. Many architects, artists, historians, and heritage advocacy groups are campaigning for the restoration of Escolta Street, which was once the premier street of the Philippines.[^cite_note-224]



The [Luneta Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luneta_Hotel), an example of [French Renaissance architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Renaissance_architecture) with Filipino stylized beaux art

Almost all of Manila's pre-war and Spanish colonial architecture was destroyed during the [1945 Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)) by intensive bombardment by the United States Air Force. Reconstruction took place afterward, replacing the destroyed historic Spanish-era buildings with modern ones, erasing much of the city's character. Some of the destroyed buildings, such as the Old Legislative Building (now the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Fine_Arts_\(Manila\))), [Ayuntamiento de Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayuntamiento_de_Manila) (now the Bureau of the Treasury), and the under-construction [San Ignacio Church and Convent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ignacio_Church_\(Manila\)) (as the [Museo de Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_de_Intramuros)), have been reconstructed. There are plans to refurbish and restore several neglected historic buildings and places such as Plaza Del Carmen, [San Sebastian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebastian_Church_\(Manila\)), and the [NCCA Metropolitan Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCCA_Metropolitan_Theater). Spanish-era shops and houses in the districts of [Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo), [Quiapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo,_Manila), and [San Nicolas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Nicolas,_Manila) are also planned to be restored as a part of a movement to restore the city to its pre-war state.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226]

Because Manila is prone to earthquakes, Spanish colonial architects invented a style called [Earthquake Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_Baroque), which churches and government buildings during the Spanish colonial period adopted.[^cite_note-City-192] As a result, succeeding earthquakes of the 18th and 19th centuries barely affected Manila, although they periodically leveled the surrounding area. Modern buildings in and around Manila are designed or have been retrofitted to withstand an 8.2 magnitude quake in accordance with the country's building code.[^cite_note-227]

Manila population pyramid in 2021



People flocking to the [Binondo Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo) during [Chinese New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Year)

According to the 2024 Philippine census, Manila has a population of 1,902,590 people, making it the [second-most-populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_the_Philippines) in the Philippines.[^cite_note-234] Manila is [the most-densely populated city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population_density) in the world, with 41,515 inhabitants per km<sup>2</sup> in 2015.[^cite_note-PSApopden-9] District 6 is listed as the densest with 68,266 inhabitants per km<sup>2</sup>, followed by District 1 with 64,936 and District 2 with 64,710. District 5 is the least-densely populated area with 19,235.[^cite_note-manilapop-235]

Manila has been presumed to be the Philippines' largest city since the establishment of a permanent Spanish settlement, and eventually became the political, commercial, and ecclesiastical capital of the country.[^cite_note-236] Since colonial times, Manila has been the destination of peoples whose origins are as wide-ranging as India.[^cite_note-237] Practicing [forensic anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forensic_anthropology), while exhuming cranial bones in several Philippine cemeteries, researcher Matthew C. Go estimated that 7% of the mean amount, among the samples exhumed, have attribution to European descent.[^cite_note-238] Research work published in the Journal of Forensic Anthropology, collating contemporary Anthropological data show that the percentage of Filipino bodies who were sampled from the [University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines), that is [phenotypically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) classified as Asian ([East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian), [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian) and [Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asian)) is 72.7%, Hispanic (Spanish-Amerindian Mestizo, [Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American), and/or Spanish-Malay [Mestizo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo)) is at 12.7%, Indigenous American ([Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas)) at 7.3%, African at 4.5%, and European at 2.7%.[^cite_note-PhilippineAnthropologyStudy-239] However, this is only according to an interpretation of the data wherein the reference groups, which were cross checked to the Filipino samples; for the Hispanic category, were [Mexican-Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican-Americans),[^cite_note-PhilippineAnthropologyStudy-239] and the reference groups for the: European, African, and Indigenous American, categories, were: [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans), [Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Americans), and [Native Americans from the USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_people), while the Asian reference groups were sourced from Chinese, Japanese, and Vietnamese origins.[^cite_note-PhilippineAnthropologyStudy-239] In contrast, a different anthropology study using Morphoscopic ancestry estimates in Filipino crania using multivariate probit regression models by J. T. Hefner, while analyzing Historic and Modern samples of Philippine skeletons, paint a different picture,[^cite_note-Hefner-240] in that, when the reference group for "Asian" was Thailand (Southeast Asians) rather than Chinese, Japanese, and Vietnamese; the reference for "Africans" included West and East Africans, along with Black Americans; and the reference group for "Hispanic" was [Colombians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_Colombia) (South Americans) rather than Mexicans,[^cite_note-Hefner-240] the historical and modern sample results for Filipinos, yielded the following ratios: Asian at 48.6%, African at 32.9%, which is attributed to extensive admixture with Negritos since the initial peopling of the Filipino archipleago, and only a small portion classifying as either European at 12.9%, and finally for Hispanic at 5.7%.[^cite_note-Hefner-240]

Between the 1860s and 1890s, in urban areas of the Philippines – especially Manila – according to burial statistics, as much as 3.3% of the population were pure European Spaniards and pure Chinese composed 9.9% of the city's populace. The Spanish-Filipino and Chinese-Filipino Mestizo populations also fluctuated, with the mixed Spanish-Filipinos composing 19% of Manila's population.[^cite_note-Estadismo1-72]<sup>: 539 </sup> Eventually, these non-native categories diminished because they were assimilated into the majority Austronesian Filipino population.[^cite_note-241] During the Philippine Revolution, the term "Filipino" included people of any race born in the Philippines.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] This explains the abrupt drop of the proportion of Chinese, Spanish, and Mestizo peoples across the country by the time of the first American census in 1903, as the foreign and mixed descended peoples identified solely as pure Filipinos.[^cite_note-244] Manila's population dramatically increased since the 1903 census because people tended to move from rural areas to towns and cities. In the 1960 census, Manila became the first Philippine city to exceed one million people – more than five times of its 1903 population. The city continued to grow until the population stabilized at 1.6 million and experienced alternating increases and decreases starting in the 1990 census year. This phenomenon may be attributed to the higher growth experienced by suburbs and the already-very-high population density of the city. As such, Manila exhibited a decreasing percentage share of the metropolitan population[^cite_note-245] from 63% in the 1950s to 27.5%[^cite_note-246] in 1980, and 13.8% in 2015. The much-larger [Quezon City](284223.md) marginally surpassed the population of Manila in 1990 and by the 2015 census it already had 1.1 million more people. Nationally, the population of Manila was expected to be overtaken by cities with larger territories such as [Caloocan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caloocan) and [Davao City](489401.md) by 2020.[^cite_note-247]

### Language

The vernacular language is [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_language), which is mostly based on the [Tagalog language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language) of the city and its surroundings, and this Manilan form of spoken Tagalog has become the *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* of the Philippines, having spread throughout the archipelago through mass media and entertainment. [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_English) is the language most widely used in education and business, and is in heavy everyday use throughout Metro Manila and the rest of the Philippines.

[Philippine Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Hokkien), which is locally known as *Lan-nang-oe*, a variant of [Southern Min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Min), is mainly spoken by the city's [Chinese-Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese-Filipino) community. According to data provided by the Bureau of Immigration, 3.12 million Chinese citizens arrived in the Philippines from January 2016 to May 2018.[^cite_note-248]

### Crime



[Manila Police District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Police_District) officers in [Rizal Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Park).

Crime in Manila is concentrated in areas that are associated with poverty, drug abuse, and gangs. Crime in the city is also directly related to its changing demographics and unique criminal justice system. The [illegal drug trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_drug_trade_in_the_Philippines) is a major problem of the city; in [Metro Manila](72340.md) alone, 92% of the barangays were affected by illegal drugs in February 2015.[^cite_note-rapplerdrugtrade-249]

From 2010 to 2015, Manila had the second-highest index crime rates in the Philippines, with 54,689 cases or an average of about 9,100 cases per year.[^cite_note-250] By October 2017, [Manila Police District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Police_District) (MPD) reported a 38.7% decrease in index crimes from 5,474 cases in 2016 to 3,393 in 2017. MPD's crime-solution efficiency also improved; six-to-seven of every ten crimes were solved by the city police force.[^cite_note-251] MPD was cited as the Best Police District in Metro Manila in 2017 for registering the highest crime-solution efficiency.[^cite_note-252]

### Religion

See also: [List of Catholic churches in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_churches_in_Metro_Manila)

Religion in Manila (circa 2010)

[^cite_note-RELIGION-253]

1. [Catholicism](606848.md) (93.5%)

2. [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md) (1.90%)

3. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (1.80%)

4. [Buddhism](3267529.md) (1.10%)

5. Other (1.40%)

#### Christianity

As a result of [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain) cultural influence, Manila is a predominantly [Christian](8276776.md) city. As of 2010, 93.5% of the population were [Roman Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines), 1.9% were adherents of the [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md), 1.8% followed various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism), and 1.1% were [Buddhists](3267529.md). Members of [Islam](6037917.md) and other religions make up the remaining 1.4% of the population.[^cite_note-RELIGION-253]

Manila is the seat of prominent Catholic churches and institutions. There are 113 Catholic churches within the city limits; 63 of which are considered major shrines, basilicas, or cathedrals.[^cite_note-254] [Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral), the country's oldest established church, is the seat of the [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Manila).[^cite_note-wow-255] There are another three basilicas in the city; [Quiapo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo_Church), [Binondo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo_Church), and the [Minor Basilica of San Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Sebastian,_Manila).[^cite_note-256] [San Agustín Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church,_Manila) in Intramuros is a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-257]

Several [Mainline Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestant) denominations are headquartered in the city. [St. Stephen's Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Stephen's_Parish_\(Manila\)) [pro-cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-cathedral) in Santa Cruz district is the [see](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_see) of the [Episcopal Church in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_in_the_Philippines)' Diocese of Central Philippines, while on Taft Avenue are the [main cathedral and central offices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_Filipina_Independiente_National_Cathedral) of *[Iglesia Filipina Independiente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_Filipina_Independiente)* (also called the Aglipayan Church), a [nationalist church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_nationalism) that is a product of the [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution). [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) (LDS Church) has a [temple within Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Philippines_Temple), one of two operating LDS temples in the Philippines.

The indigenous [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md) has several locales (akin to parishes) in the city, including its first chapel, now [a museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_ni_Cristo_Museum_\(Santa_Ana,_Manila\)), in Punta, Santa Ana.[^cite_note-258] [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Christian), [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal) and [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist) denominations also thrive. The headquarters of the Philippine Bible Society is in Manila. The main campus of the [Cathedral of Praise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Praise) is located on Taft Avenue. [Jesus Is Lord Church Worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Is_Lord_Church_Worldwide) has several branches and campuses in Manila.

Religious groups such as [Members Church of God International (MCGI)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Members_Church_of_God_International),[^cite_note-259] Iglesia ni Cristo, Jesus Is Lord Church Worldwide, and the [El Shaddai movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Shaddai_\(movement\)) celebrate their anniversaries at Quirino Grandstand, which is an open space in Rizal Park.[^cite_note-260]

- Manila Cathedral is the seat of Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Manila.

  [Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral) is the seat of [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Manila).

- The Minor Basilica of San Sebastián is the only all-steel church in Asia.

  The [Minor Basilica of San Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Sebastian,_Manila) is the only [all-steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_building) church in Asia.

  [^cite_note-whl-261]

- San Agustín Church in Intramuros, a UNESCO World Heritage Site

  [San Agustín Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church,_Manila) in [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros), a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md)

- Binondo Church serves the Roman Catholic Chinese community.

  [Binondo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo_Church) serves the Roman Catholic Chinese community.

- Quiapo Church, home of the iconic Black Nazarene, whose Traslacion feast is celebrated every January 9

  [Quiapo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo_Church), home of the iconic [Black Nazarene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Nazarene), whose *Traslacion* feast is celebrated every January 9

- The Iglesia ni Cristo (INC) Lokal ng Tondo, established on November 6, 1915, is one of the earliest congregations of the church. The current structure was completed in 1967.

  The [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md) (INC) Lokal ng Tondo, established on November 6, 1915, is one of the earliest congregations of the church. The [current structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iglesia_ni_Cristo_chapel,_Tondo) was completed in 1967.

- Seng Guan Temple

  [Seng Guan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seng_Guan_Temple)

#### Other faiths

Manila has many [Taoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoist) and Buddhist temples like [Seng Guan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seng_Guan_Temple) that serve the spiritual needs of the [Chinese Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Filipino) community.[^cite_note-262] Quiapo has a "[Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_Philippines) town" that includes the city's largest mosque [Masjid Al-Dahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_Al-Dahab).[^cite_note-263] Members of the Indian expatriate community can worship at the large [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_the_Philippines) temple in the city or at the [Sikh](27964.md) [gurdwara](482849.md) on [United Nations Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Avenue). The [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)'s governing body in the Philippines the [National Spiritual Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_Assembly) is headquartered near Manila's eastern boundary with Makati.

## Economy
