&#x20;**Source:** [Philippine Statistics Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Statistics_Authority)[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272]&#x20;



Skyline of [Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo), the central business district of the city of Manila.

Manila is a major center for commerce, banking and finance, retailing, transportation, tourism, real estate, [new media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_media), [traditional media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_media), advertising, legal services, accounting, insurance, theater, fashion, and the arts. Around 60,000 establishments operate in the city.[^cite_note-273] In 2024, Manila is the [4th largest economy in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_of_the_Philippines_by_GDP), with a 4.7% share to the national gross domestic product totaling ₱1.04 trillion.[^cite_note-PSA-10LargerstEconomy-274] Manila has the 7th highest per capita GDP of the country at [₱](227672.md)510,826 ([US\$](18717338.md)9,500).[^cite_note-GDP-275]

The National Competitiveness Council of the Philippines, which annually publishes the Cities and Municipalities Competitiveness Index (CMCI), ranks the country's cities, municipalities, and provinces according to their economic dynamism, government efficiency, and infrastructure. According to the 2022 CMCI, Manila was the second-most-competitive [highly urbanized city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highly_urbanized_city) in the Philippines.[^cite_note-276] Manila held the title of the country's most-competitive city in 2015, and since then has been in the top three, denoting Manila is consistently one of the best place to live in and do business.[^cite_note-277] The city has an estimated [GDP](12594.md) of ₱987.88 billion[^cite_note-278] and is the 3rd largest economy of the [National Capital Region](72340.md), accounting for 15% of the region's total economy as of 2023.[^cite_note-279]

[Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo), the oldest and one of the largest [Chinatowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown) in the world, was the center of commerce and business activities in the city. Numerous residential and office skyscrapers occupy its medieval streets. As of 2013, plans by the city government of Manila to turn the Chinatown area into a [business process outsourcing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_process_outsourcing) (BPO) hub were in progress; thirty unoccupied buildings had been already identified for conversion into BPO offices. Most of these buildings are on [Escolta Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escolta_Street), Binondo.[^cite_note-280]



View of the [Manila International Container Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manila), the chief port of the Philippines

The [Port of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manila) is the largest seaport in the Philippines and the main international shipping route into the country. The [Philippine Ports Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Ports_Authority) oversees the operation and management of the country's ports. [International Container Terminal Services Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Container_Terminal_Services_Inc.), according to the [Asian Development Bank](2512.md), is one of the top-five major maritime terminal operators in the world,[^cite_note-pse-281][^cite_note-forbes-282] and has its headquarters and main operations at the Port of Manila. Another port operator, Asian Terminal Incorporated, has its corporate office and main operations at [Manila South Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_South_Harbor#Manila_South_Harbor), and its container depository is in [Santa Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa). Manila is classified as a Medium-Port Megacity, using the Southampton system for port-city classification.[^cite_note-283]

Manufacturers within the city produce industrial-related products such as chemicals, textiles, clothing, electronic goods, food, beverages, and tobacco products. Local businesses process primary commodities for export, including rope, plywood, refined sugar, [copra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copra), and coconut oil. The food-processing industry is one of the most-stable manufacturing sector in the city.[^cite_note-284]



*[Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas](1276593.md)* headquarters

[Pandacan oil depot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandacan_oil_depot) houses the storage facilities and distribution terminals of [Caltex Philippines](284749.md), [Pilipinas Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Dutch_Shell), and [Petron Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petron_Corporation); the major players in the country's petroleum industry. The oil depot has been a subject of various concerns, including its environmental and health impact on the residents of Manila. The Supreme Court ordered the oil depot to be relocated outside the city by July 2015,[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] but it failed to meet this deadline. Most of the oil depot facility inside the 33-hectare (82-acre) compound were demolished,[^cite_note-287] and plans have been made to convert it into a transport hub or food park.[^cite_note-288]

Manila is a major publishing center of the Philippines.[^cite_note-encarta-289] *[Manila Bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bulletin)*, the Philippines' largest broadsheet newspaper by circulation, is headquartered in Intramuros.[^cite_note-Manila_Bulletin-290] Other major publishing companies in the country *[The Manila Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Manila_Times)*, *[The Philippine Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Philippine_Star)*, and *[Manila Standard Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Standard_Today)* are headquartered in the Port Area. The *[Chinese Commercial News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Commercial_News)*, the Philippines' oldest existing Chinese-language newspaper, and the country's third-oldest newspaper,[^cite_note-memorial-291] is headquartered in Binondo. [DWRK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DWRK) used to have its studio at the FEMS Tower 1 along [Osmeña Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osme%C3%B1a_Highway) in [Malate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila) before transferring to the [MBC Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Broadcasting_Company) at the [CCP Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CCP_Complex) in 2008.[^cite_note-292]

Manila serves as the headquarters of the [Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas](1276593.md), which is located on [Roxas Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxas_Boulevard).[^cite_note-BSP-293] The [Landbank of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landbank_of_the_Philippines) and [Philippine Trust Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Trust_Company) also have their headquarters in Manila. [Unilever Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unilever) used to have its corporate office on [United Nations Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Avenue) in [Paco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paco,_Manila) before transferring to [Bonifacio Global City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonifacio_Global_City) in 2016.[^cite_note-Unilever_Phils-294] Vehicle manufacturer [Toyota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota) also has its regional office on UN Avenue.

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Metro_Manila)



The historic [Plaza Moriones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Moriones,_Intramuros) with the [Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral) in the background.

Manila welcomes over one million tourists each year.[^cite_note-encarta-289] Major tourist destinations include the historic Walled City of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros), the [Cultural Center of the Philippines Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Center_of_the_Philippines_Complex),[^cite_note-Manila_Note_II-295] [Manila Ocean Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Ocean_Park), Binondo (Chinatown), Ermita, [Malate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila), [Manila Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Zoo), the [National Museum Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_Complex_\(Manila\)), and [Rizal Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Park).[^cite_note-296] Both the historic Walled City of Intramuros and Rizal Park were designated as flagship destinations and as tourism enterprise zones in the [Tourism Act of 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_laws#2009_\(9519%E2%80%939999\)).[^cite_note-TourismAct-297]

Rizal Park, also known as Luneta Park, is a national park and the largest urban park in Asia.[^cite_note-298] with an area of 58 hectares (140 acres),[^cite_note-299] The park was constructed to honor of the country's national hero [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal), who was executed by the Spaniards on charges of subversion. The flagpole west of the Rizal Monument is the [Kilometer Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilometre_Zero) marker for distances to locations across the country. The park is managed by the National Parks and Development Committee.[^cite_note-300]

The 0.67-square-kilometer (0.26 sq mi) Walled City of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) is the historic center of Manila. It is administered by the [Intramuros Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros_Administration), an attached agency of the [Department of Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Tourism_\(Philippines\)). It contains [Manila Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Cathedral) and the 18th Century [San Agustin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church_\(Manila\)), a UNESCO World Heritage Site. [Kalesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalesa) is a popular mode of transportation for tourists in Intramuros and nearby places including Binondo, Ermita and Rizal Park.[^cite_note-301] Binondo, the oldest Chinatown in the world, was established in 1521[^cite_note-302] and served as a hub of Chinese commerce before the Spaniards colonized the Philippines. Its main attractions are [Binondo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo_Church), [Filipino-Chinese Friendship Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino-Chinese_Friendship_Arch), Seng Guan Buddhist Temple, and authentic Chinese restaurants.

Manila is designated as the country's leading destination for [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism), which is estimated to annually generate \$1 billion in revenue.[^cite_note-303] Lack of a progressive health system, inadequate infrastructure, and the unstable political environment are seen as hindrances to its growth.[^cite_note-304]

### Shopping



The old [Tutuban Main Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_Center) built in 1892, which is now converted to a shopping mall



[Divisoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisoria) is a popular flea market for locals and tourists. Shown is the interior of [168 Shopping Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/168_Shopping_Mall).

Manila is regarded as one of the best shopping destinations in Asia.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] Major shopping malls, department stores, markets, supermarkets, and bazaars are located within the city.

[Divisoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisoria) in Tondo has been locally described as a "shopping mecca" of Manila.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] Shopping malls sell goods at bargain prices. Small vendors occupy several roads, causing pedestrian and vehicular traffic. A well-known landmark in Divisoria is the [Tutuban Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_Center), a large shopping mall that is a part of the [Philippine National Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Railways)' [Main Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_railway_station). It attracts 1 million people every month and is expected to add another 400,000 people upon the completion of the [LRT Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Light_Rail_Transit_System_Line_2) West Extension, making it Manila's busiest transfer station.[^cite_note-309] Another "lifestyle mall" is [Lucky Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucky_Chinatown). There are almost 1 million shoppers in Divisoria according to the Manila Police District.[^cite_note-310]

Binondo, the oldest Chinatown in the world,[^cite_note-en.radio86.com-63] is the city's center of commerce and trade for all types of businesses run by Filipino-Chinese merchants, with a wide variety of shops and restaurants. [Quiapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo,_Manila) is referred to as the "Old Downtown", where [tiangges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiangge), markets, boutique shops, music and electronics stores are common.[^cite_note-311] Many department stores are on [Recto Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recto_Avenue).

[Robinsons Place Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinsons_Place_Manila) is Manila's largest shopping mall.[^cite_note-312] The mall was the second and the largest [Robinsons Malls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinsons_Malls) built. [SM Supermalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SM_Supermalls) operates the shopping malls SM City Manila and [SM City San Lazaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SM_City_San_Lazaro). SM City Manila is located on the former site of YMCA Manila beside [Manila City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Hall) in [Ermita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ermita), while SM City San Lazaro is built on the site of the former [San Lazaro Hippodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Lazaro_Hippodrome) in [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Manila). The building of the former Manila Royal Hotel in Quiapo, which is known for its revolving restaurant, is now the [SM Clearance Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SM_Quiapo) and was established in 1972.[^cite_note-313] The site of the first SM Department Store is Carlos Palanca Sr. (formerly Echague) Street in [San Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel,_Manila).[^cite_note-314]

## Culture

### Museums



The [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Fine_Arts_\(Manila\))

As the cultural center of the Philippines, Manila has a number of museums. The [National Museum Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_Complex_\(Manila\)) of the [National Museum of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_the_Philippines), located in Rizal Park, is composed of the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Fine_Arts_\(Manila\)), the [National Museum of Anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Anthropology_\(Manila\)), the [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History_\(Manila\)),[^cite_note-315] and the [National Planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Planetarium_\(Manila\)). *[Spoliarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoliarium)*, a famous painting by [Juan Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Luna), can be found in the complex.[^cite_note-316]

The city hosts the [National Library of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_the_Philippines), a repository of the country's printed and recorded cultural heritage, and other literary and information resources.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] The [National Historical Commission of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historical_Commission_of_the_Philippines) maintains two history museums in the city, which are the [Museo ni Apolinario Mabini – PUP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabini_Shrine_\(Manila\)) and the [Museo ni Jose Rizal – Fort Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Shrine_\(Intramuros\)).[^cite_note-319] Museums established or run by the National Library and by educational institutions such as DLS-CSB Museum of Contemporary Art and Design,[^cite_note-320] [UST Museum of Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UST_Museum_of_Arts_and_Sciences),[^cite_note-321] and the [UP Museum of a History of Ideas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines_Museum_of_a_History_of_Ideas) are located in the city.[^cite_note-322]



[National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History_\(Manila\)) at Agrifina Circle, [Rizal Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Park)

[Bahay Tsinoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_Tsinoy), one of Manila's prominent museums, documents the lives of Chinese people and their contributions to the history of the Philippines.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] Intramuros Light and Sound Museum chronicles Filipinos' desire for freedom during [the revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution) under Rizal's leadership and other revolutionary leaders. The [Metropolitan Museum of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Manila) houses modern and contemporary visual arts, and exhibits Filipino arts and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-325]

Other museums in the city are the [Museo Pambata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Pambata),[^cite_note-326] a children's museum;[^cite_note-327] and Plaza San Luis, an outdoor heritage public museum that includes nine Spanish [Bahay na Bato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_na_Bato) houses.[^cite_note-328] Ecclesiastical museums located in the city are the Parish of the Our Lady of the Abandoned in Santa Ana;[^cite_note-329] [San Agustin Church Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church_\(Manila\));[^cite_note-330] and the [Museo de Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_de_Intramuros), which houses the ecclesiastical art collection of the [Intramuros Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros_Administration) in the reconstructed [San Ignacio Church and Convent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ignacio_Church_\(Manila\)).[^cite_note-331]

### Sports



Ground view of the city-owned [Rizal Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Stadium), part of the [Rizal Memorial Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Sports_Complex).



Children playing basketball at the ruins of [San Ignacio Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ignacio_Church_\(Manila\)) in Intramuros

Sports in Manila have a long and distinguished history. The city's, and in general the country's, main sport is [basketball](3921.md). Most barangays have a basketball court or a makeshift one, and court markings are frequently drawn on the streets. Larger barangays have covered courts where inter-barangay leagues are held every April to May. Manila's major sports venues include [Rizal Memorial Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Sports_Complex) and San Andres Gym, the base of the now-defunct [Manila Metrostars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Metrostars).[^cite_note-332]

Rizal Memorial Sports Complex houses [a track and football stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Track_and_Football_Stadium), [a baseball stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Baseball_Stadium), tennis courts, [Rizal Memorial Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Coliseum), and [Ninoy Aquino Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino_Stadium); the latter two are indoor arenas. The Rizal complex had hosted several multi-sport events, such as the [1954 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Asian_Games) and the [1934 Far Eastern Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1934_Far_Eastern_Games). When the Philippines hosts the [Southeast Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asian_Games), most of the events are held at the complex but in the [2005 Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Southeast_Asian_Games), most events were held elsewhere. The [1960 ABC Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_ABC_Championship) and the [1973 ABC Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_ABC_Championship), forerunners of the [FIBA Asia Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Asia_Championship), were hosted at the memorial coliseum; the [national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_men's_national_basketball_team) won both tournaments.[^cite_note-333] The [1978 FIBA World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_FIBA_World_Championship) was held at the coliseum although the latter stages were held in the [Araneta Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Araneta_Coliseum) in Quezon City.

Manila has several other well-known sports facilities such as [Enrique M. Razon Sports Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_M._Razon_Sports_Center) and the [University of Santo Tomas Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas_Sports_Complex), both of which are private venues owned by a university; [collegiate sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegiate_sports) are also held in the city; the [University Athletic Association of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Athletic_Association_of_the_Philippines) and the [National Collegiate Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Collegiate_Athletic_Association_\(Philippines\)) basketball games held at Rizal Memorial Coliseum and Ninoy Aquino Stadium, although basketball events have been transferred to [San Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan,_Metro_Manila)'s [Filoil Flying V Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filoil_Flying_V_Arena) and Araneta Coliseum in Quezon City.

Other collegiate sports are still held at Rizal Memorial Sports Complex. Professional basketball, which has been mostly organized by corporate teams, also used to play at the city but the [Philippine Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Basketball_Association) now holds their games at Araneta Coliseum and [Cuneta Astrodome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneta_Astrodome) at [Pasay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasay); the now-defunct [Philippine Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Basketball_League) played some of their games, such as its [1995–96 Philippine Basketball League season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995%E2%80%9396_Philippine_Basketball_League_season), at Rizal Memorial Sports Complex.[^cite_note-334]

[Manila Metrostars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Metrostars) participated in the [Metropolitan Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Basketball_Association).[^cite_note-335] The Metrostars, named after the [Metrostar Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrostar_Express) – the brand name of the [Metro Manila MRT-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRT_Line_3_\(Metro_Manila\)), which does not have stations in the city – participated in its first three seasons and won the 1999 championship.[^cite_note-336] The Metrostars later merged with the [Batangas Blades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batangas_Blades) and subsequently played in [Lipa, Batangas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipa,_Batangas). Almost twenty years later, [Manila Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Stars) participated in the [Maharlika Pilipinas Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharlika_Pilipinas_Basketball_League), reaching the Northern Division Finals in 2019. Both teams played in the San Andres Sports Complex. Other teams that represented Manila but did not host games in the city are the [Manila Jeepney F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Jeepney_F.C.) and [FC Meralco Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_F.C.). The city's government acknowledged Jeepney as Manila's representative in the [United Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Football_League_\(Philippines\)). Meralco Manila played in the [Philippines Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_Football_League) and designated Rizal Memorial Stadium as their home ground.

Manila's [rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_league) team [Manila Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Storm) trains at Rizal Park and plays matches at Southern Plains Field, [Calamba, Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamba,_Laguna). Baseball was previously a widely played sport in the city but in 2022, Manila had the Philippines' only sizable baseball stadium, [Rizal Memorial Baseball Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Baseball_Stadium), which hosted games of the now-defunct [Baseball Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_Philippines); [Lou Gehrig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lou_Gehrig) and [Babe Ruth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babe_Ruth) were the first players to score a [home run](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_run) at the stadium during their tour of the country on December 2, 1934.[^cite_note-337] [Cue sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cue_sports) are also popular in Manila; billiard halls are present in most barangays. The [2010 World Cup of Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_World_Cup_of_Pool) was held at [Robinsons Place Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinsons_Place_Manila).[^cite_note-338]

[Rizal Memorial Track and Football Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Memorial_Track_and_Football_Stadium) hosted the first [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) qualifier in decades when the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_national_football_team) hosted [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka_national_basketball_team) in July 2011. The stadium, which was previously unfit for international matches, had been renovated before the match.[^cite_note-339] The stadium also hosted its first [rugby test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Test_match_\(rugby_union\)) for the [2012 Asian Five Nations Division I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Asian_Five_Nations_division_tournaments) tournaments.[^cite_note-340]

### Festivals and holidays

Further information: [Public holidays in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_the_Philippines)



Catholic devotees during the [Feast of the Black Nazarene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Nazarene) (*Traslacíon*)

Manila celebrates civic and national holidays. Because most of the city's residents are Roman Catholic,[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] most of the festivals are religious in nature. Araw ng Maynila, which celebrates the city's founding on June 24, 1571[^cite_note-343] by the Spanish *[conquistador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador)* [Miguel López de Legazpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_L%C3%B3pez_de_Legazpi), was first proclaimed by the city's vice mayor [Herminio A. Astorga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herminio_A._Astorga) in June 1962. It has been annually commemorated under the patronage of [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist), and has always been declared by the national government as a special, non-working holiday through presidential proclamations. Each of the city's 896 barangays have their own festivities, which are guided by their own [patron saints](68055.md).

Manila also hosts the [procession of the Feast of the Black Nazarene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Nazarene) (*Traslacíon*), which is held every January 9 and draws millions of Catholic followers.[^cite_note-344] Other religious festivities held in Manila are the [Feast of Santo Niño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_Ni%C3%B1o_de_Ceb%C3%BA) in Tondo and Pandacan, which is held on the third Sunday of January;[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] the Feast of *Nuestra Señora de los Desamparados de Manila* (Our Lady of the Abandoned), the patron saint of [Santa Ana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana,_Manila), which is held every May 12;[^cite_note-347] and the *[Flores de Mayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flores_de_Mayo)*.[^cite_note-348] Non-religious holidays include [New Year's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year's_Day), [National Heroes' Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Heroes'_Day), [Bonifacio Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonifacio_Day), and [Rizal Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Day).[^cite_note-349]

## Government

### Local government

Main article: [Sangguniang Panglungsod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangguniang_Panglungsod)



[Manila City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Hall), the seat of city government



The inaugural session of the 12th [Manila City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Council) at the city hall, 2022

Manila, which is officially known as the City of Manila, is the [national capital](181337.md) of the Philippines and is classified as a special city according to its income,[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] and a [highly urbanized city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_the_Philippines#classification) (HUC). The [Mayor of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Manila) is the chief executive, and is assisted by the vice mayor and the 38-member [City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_City_Council), who are elected as representatives of the six councilor districts within the city, and the municipal presidents of the [Liga ng mga Barangay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_ng_mga_Barangay) and [Sangguniang Kabataan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangguniang_Kabataan).

The city has no control over [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) and [Manila North Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manila). The historic Walled City is administered by the [Intramuros Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros_Administration) while Manila North Harbor is managed by the [Philippine Ports Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Ports_Authority). Both are national government agencies. The barangays that have jurisdictions over these places oversee the welfare of the city's constituents but cannot exercise their executive powers. Manila had a 12,971 personnel complement at the end of 2018.[^cite_note-2019audit-352] Under the proposed form of [federalism in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_in_the_Philippines), Manila may no longer be the capital and Metro Manila may no longer be the seat of government; the committee has not yet decided on the federal capital and states they are open to other proposals.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354]

As of June 2025, the mayor is [Isko Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isko_Moreno), who is on his second mayoral stint. The vice mayor is [Chi Atienza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chi_Atienza), daughter of former mayor [Lito Atienza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lito_Atienza). The mayor and the vice mayor are limited to up-to three terms, each term lasting for three years. The city has an ordinance penalizing [cat-calling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_harassment) since 2018, and is the second city in the Philippines to do so after Quezon City, which passed a similar ordinance in 2016.[^cite_note-355] In 2017, the city government planned to revise the existing curfew ordinance since the Supreme Court declared it unconstitutional in August that year. Of the three cities reviewed by the Supreme Court; the City of Manila, [Navotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navotas), and Quezon City; only the curfew ordinance of Quezon City was approved.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357]

### National government



[Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace), the [official residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_residence) and workplace of the [President of the Philippines](271126.md).



The [Palacio del Gobernador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_del_Gobernador) in Intramuros is home to the Philippine [Commission on Elections](1847922.md) and Intramuros Administration.

Manila, being the seat of political power in the Philippines, has the headquarters of several national government offices. Planning for the city's role as the center of government started during the early years of [American colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1898%E2%80%931946\)), when the U.S. envisioned a well-designed city outside the walls of Intramuros, and chose Bagumbayan, a former town that is now Jose Rizal Park to become the center of government. A design commission was given to [Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) to create a master plan for the city patterned after [Washington, D.C.](108956.md)[^cite_note-Morley-2018-358] but the plans were abandoned under the Commonwealth Government of [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon).

A new government center was to be built on the hills northeast of Manila, in what is now Quezon City. Several government agencies have set up their headquarters in Quezon City. Several key government offices are still based in Manila. Many of the plans were substantially altered after the devastation of Manila during World War II and by subsequent administrations.

As the nation's capital, Manila hosts the [Office of the President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_President_of_the_Philippines) and the President's [official residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace). It also houses important government agencies and institutions such as the [Supreme Court](1335534.md), the [Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Court_of_Appeals), the [Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas](1276593.md), the [Departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Departments_of_the_Philippines) of [Budget and Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Budget_and_Management), [Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Finance_\(Philippines\)), [Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_\(Philippines\)), [Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Justice_\(Philippines\)), [Labor and Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Labor_and_Employment_\(Philippines\)), and [Public Works and Highways](4701882.md). Manila also hosts important national institutions such as the National Library, National Archives, [National Museum of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_the_Philippines), and [Philippine General Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_General_Hospital). Other notable institutions based in Manila are the [National Commission for Culture and the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Commission_for_Culture_and_the_Arts), [National Historical Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historical_Commission), [Film Development Council of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_Development_Council_of_the_Philippines), and the [Cultural Center of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Center_of_the_Philippines).



The facade of the [Supreme Court of the Philippines](1335534.md).

Congress previously held office at the [Old Congress Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Legislative_Building_\(Manila\)).[^cite_note-359] In 1972, due to declaration of martial law, Congress was dissolved; its successor, the [unicameral](204304.md) [Batasang Pambansa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batasang_Pambansa), held office at the new [Batasang Pambansa Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batasang_Pambansa_Complex). When a new [constitution](19554336.md) restored the [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral) Congress, the House of Representatives stayed at the Batasang Pambansa Complex and the Senate remained at the Old Congress Building. In May 1997, the Senate transferred to a new building, which it shares with the [Government Service Insurance System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Service_Insurance_System) on reclaimed land at [Pasay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasay). The [Supreme Court](1335534.md) was due to transfer to its new campus at [Bonifacio Global City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonifacio_Global_City), [Taguig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taguig), in 2019 but the move was postponed to a later year.[^cite_note-360]

In Congress, Manila has six representatives, one each from [its six congressional districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_districts_of_Manila).[^cite_note-361]

### Finance

In the 2019 Annual Audit Report published by the [Commission on Audit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Audit_\(Philippines\)), the revenue of the City of Manila was ₱16.534 billion.[^cite_note-2019audit-352] It is one of the cities with the highest tax collection and internal revenue allotment.[^cite_note-362] For the 2019 fiscal year, the tax revenue collected by the city was ₱8.4 billion. The city's Internal Revenue Allotment (IRA) from the National Treasury was ₱2.94 billion, and the city's total assets were worth ₱63.4 billion in 2019.[^cite_note-2019audit-352] The City of Manila has the highest budget allocation for healthcare of all the cities and municipalities in the Philippines; the city maintains the six district hospitals, 59 health centers and lying-in clinics, and healthcare programs.

## Infrastructure

### Housing



[Smokey Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokey_Mountain) Housing Project was built on a former landfill. Continuous development of housing buildings continues up to the present day.

Development of public housing in Manila began in the 1930s under U.S. rule. Americans had to deal with the problem of sanitation and concentration of settlers around business areas.[^cite_note-363] Business codes and sanitation laws were implemented in the 1930s. During this period until the 1950s, new communities were opened for relocation. Among these were Projects 1–8 in Quezon City[^cite_note-364] and the Vitas tenement houses in Tondo.[^cite_note-365] In 1947, the government implemented a public housing policy that established the People's Homesite and Housing Corporation (PHHC).[^cite_note-366] A few years later, it established a Slum Clearance Committee which, with the help of the PHHC, relocated thousands of families from Manila and Quezon City to Sapang Palay in [San Jose del Monte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose_del_Monte), Bulacan in the 1960s.[^cite_note-367]

In 2016, the national government completed several medium-rise houses for 300 Manila residents whose slum community was destroyed by a fire in 2011.[^cite_note-368] As of 2019, the city government plans to retrofit dilapidated tenements within the city,[^cite_note-369] and will construct new housing buildings for the city's informal settlers such as the 14-story Tondominium 1 and Tondomium 2 buildings, containing 42-square-meter (450 sq ft), two-bedroom units. The construction of these new in-city vertical housing projects was funded by a loan from the [Development Bank of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_Bank_of_the_Philippines) and the [Land Bank of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Bank_of_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371]

Since 2019, the Manila City Government has initiated six housing projects: Tondominium 1 & 2, Binondominium, Basecommunity, San Lazaro Residences, Pedro Gil Residences, and San Sebastian Residences.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374]

### Transportation

Main articles: [Transportation in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Metro_Manila), [Rail transportation in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transportation_in_Metro_Manila), and [Major roads in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_roads_in_Metro_Manila)



[Jeepneys](884965.md) are one of the most popular modes of transportation in Manila.



[Pureza station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pureza_station) of [LRT Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LRT_Line_2_\(Metro_Manila\)) in [Santa Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa)



[Carriedo station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carriedo_station) of the [LRT Line 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LRT_Line_1_\(Metro_Manila\))

One of the best-known modes of transportation in Manila is the [jeepney](884965.md), which were patterned after [U.S. Army jeeps](15658.md#World_War_II_Jeeps) and have been in use since the mid-to-late 1940s.[^cite_note-375] The [Tamaraw FX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamaraw_FX), the third generation of the [Toyota Kijang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Kijang), once directly competed with jeepneys and followed fixed routes for a set price. They were replaced by the [UV Express](40666464.md). All types of public road transportation in Manila are privately owned and operated under government-issued franchises.

On a for-hire basis, the city is served by [taxicabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxicab), "[tricycles](29843385.md)" – motorcycles with sidecars—the Philippine version of the [auto rickshaw](593765.md), and "*trisikads*", "*sikads*" or "*kuligligs*"; bicycles with sidecars, the Philippine version of [pedicabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycle_rickshaw), which are popular in some areas, especially Divisoria. Spanish-era horse-drawn [calesas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calesa) are a popular tourist attraction and mode of transportation in Binondo and Intramuros. Manila will phase out all gasoline-run tricycles and pedicabs, and replace them with electric tricycles (e-trikes), and plans to distribute 10,000 e-trikes to qualified tricycle drivers from the city.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] By January 2018, the city has distributed e-trikes to a number of drivers and operators in Binondo, Ermita, [Malate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila), and Santa Cruz.[^cite_note-378]

Manila is serviced by [LRT Line 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LRT_Line_1_\(Metro_Manila\)) (LRT-1) and [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LRT_Line_2_\(Metro_Manila\)) (LRT-2), which form the [Manila Light Rail Transit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Light_Rail_Transit_System). Development of the light rail system began in the 1970s during the presidency of Ferdinand Marcos, when the LRT Line 1 was built, making it the first light-rail system in Southeast Asia. Despite its name, LRT-1 operates as a [light metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_metro), running on dedicated rights-of-way. LRT 2 operates as a full-metro, heavy rail system. As of 2015, these systems were undergoing a multi-billion-dollar expansion.[^cite_note-10points-379] The LRT runs along the length of [Taft Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft_Avenue) (N170/R-2) and [Rizal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Avenue) (N150/R-9), while LRT-2 runs along [Claro M. Recto Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recto_Avenue) (N145/C-1) and [Ramon Magsaysay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magsaysay_Boulevard) Boulevard (N180/R-6) from Santa Cruz, through Quezon City, and to Masinag in [Antipolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipolo), [Rizal](232458.md).



A [PNR 8000 class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNR_8000_class) at [Santa Mesa station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mesa_station), the fourth station southbound from [Tutuban station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_station) terminus.

[Tutuban station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuban_station), the central terminal of the [Philippine National Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Railways), lies within Manila.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381] Within Metro Manila, one commuter railway is in operation. The line runs in a general north–south direction from Tutuban (Tondo) toward the province of [Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_\(province\)). The Port of Manila, which is located in the western section of the city on Manila Bay, is the largest and chief seaport of the Philippines.[^cite_note-382] The [Pasig River Ferry Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasig_River_Ferry_Service) is another form of transportation.[^cite_note-383] The city is also served by [Ninoy Aquino International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino_International_Airport), the country's main international airport and domestic air hub.[^cite_note-384]

Trolleys, hand-made human-powered metal handcarts operated by "trolley boys", transport people along sections of the PNR lines. This is a popular means of transportation because it is low-cost – roughly ₱10 or US\$.20 per trip – and avoids traffic. Many trolley boys are homeless and live alongside the railroad line, which is actively used by passenger trains, making collisions with passenger trains a consistent danger, although casualties are rare. The trolley rides are unofficial and unregulated but tolerated by authorities.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388]

Satellite navigation company [TomTom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TomTom) ranked Manila as the second world's most-traffic-congested city in 2019.[^cite_note-389] According to [Waze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waze)'s 2015 "Global Driver Satisfaction Index", Manila has the worst traffic worldwide.[^cite_note-390] Manila is notorious for its frequent [traffic jams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_jam) and high densities.[^cite_note-391] The government has undertaken several projects to alleviate the traffic in the city, some of which include the proposed construction of a new viaduct or underpass at the intersection of [España Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espa%C3%B1a_Boulevard) and [Lacson Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacson_Avenue);[^cite_note-392] the construction of [Skyway Stage 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Manila_Skyway#Stage_3), and [NLEX Connector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NLEX_Connector); the proposed LRT Line 2 West Extension Project from Recto Avenue to Pier 4 of Manila North Harbor;[^cite_note-393] the construction of the [North–South Commuter Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_Commuter_Railway) (NSCR);[^cite_note-394] the proposed construction of the PNR East–West line ([MRT Line 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRT_Line_8_\(Metro_Manila\))) through España Boulevard to Quezon City; and the expansion and widening of several national and local roads. These projects had yet to make any meaningful impact by 2014, and the traffic jams and congestion continue.[^cite_note-395]

The government, under its 2014 [Metro Manila Dream Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Manila_Dream_Plan) aims to address these urban transport problems. The plan is a list of short-term priority projects and medium-to-long-term infrastructure projects that will last up to 2030.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

### Water and electricity

Water services used to be provided by the [Metropolitan Waterworks and Sewerage System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Waterworks_and_Sewerage_System) (MWSS), which served 30% of the city. Most other sewage was directly dumped into storm drains, septic tanks, and open canals.[^cite_note-398] MWSS was [privatized in 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_privatization_in_Metro_Manila), which split the water concession into east and west zones. [Maynilad Water Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynilad_Water_Services) took over the west zone, of which Manila is a part.[^cite_note-399]

As of 2001, Maynilad Water Services provides the supply and delivery of potable water, and sewerage system in Manila.[^cite_note-400] The southeastern part of the city, which belongs to the east zone, is served by [Manila Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Water).[^cite_note-401] Electricity services are provided by [Meralco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meralco), the sole electricity distributor in Metro Manila.[^cite_note-402]

## Healthcare

See also: [List of hospitals in Metro Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Metro_Manila)



[Philippine General Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_General_Hospital), established in 1910, is the largest modern tertiary hospital in the country.

[^cite_note-403]

[Manila Health Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Health_Department) is responsible for the planning and implementation of healthcare programs provided by the city government. Manila Health Department operates 59 health centers and six city-run hospitals, which are free of charge for the city's constituents. The six public city-run hospitals are [Ospital ng Maynila Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ospital_ng_Maynila_Medical_Center), Ospital ng Sampaloc, Gat Andres Bonifacio Memorial Medical Center, Ospital ng Tondo, [Santa Ana Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana_Hospital_\(Manila\)), and Justice Jose Abad Santos General Hospital.[^cite_note-404] [Philippine General Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_General_Hospital), a tertiary state-owned hospital in Manila, is operated by the [University of the Philippines Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines_Manila). The city is planning to build an education, research, and hospital facility for [cleft lip and cleft palate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleft_lip_and_cleft_palate) patients,[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] and to establish the first children's surgical hospital in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-407]

Private corporations also provide healthcare in Manila. Private hospitals that operate in the city are [Manila Doctors Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Doctors_Hospital),[^cite_note-408] [Chinese General Hospital and Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_General_Hospital_and_Medical_Center),[^cite_note-409] [José R. Reyes Memorial Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_R._Reyes_Memorial_Medical_Center),[^cite_note-410] [Metropolitan Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Medical_Center),[^cite_note-411] [Our Lady of Lourdes Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Lourdes_Hospital_\(Manila\)),[^cite_note-412] and the [University of Santo Tomas Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas_Hospital).[^cite_note-413]

The [Department of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_\(Philippines\)) (DOH) has its main office in Manila[^cite_note-414] and operates San Lazaro Hospital, a special referral tertiary hospital. DOH also operates [Dr. Jose Fabella Memorial Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Jose_Fabella_Memorial_Hospital), [Jose R. Reyes Memorial Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_R._Reyes_Memorial_Medical_Center), and [Tondo Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_Medical_Center).[^cite_note-415] Manila is the home to the headquarters of the [World Health Organization](33583.md)'s Regional Office for the Western Pacific and Country Office for the Philippines.[^cite_note-416]

The city government provides free immunization programs for children, who are specifically targeted against hepatitis B, hemophilus influenza B pneumonia, diphtheria, tetanus, polio, measles, mumps, and rubella. As of 2016, 31,115 children aged one and below have been fully immunized.[^cite_note-417] Manila Dialysis Center, which provides free services for the poor, has been cited by the United Nations Committee on Innovation, Competitiveness and Public-Private Partnerships as a model for public-private partnership (PPP) projects.[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419] The dialysis facility was named Flora V. Valisno de Siojo Dialysis Center in 2019, and was inaugurated as the largest free dialysis facility in the Philippines. It has 91 dialysis machines, which can be expanded up to 100, matching the capabilities of the [National Kidney and Transplant Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Kidney_and_Transplant_Institute) (NKTI).[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421]

## Education

Main articles: [List of universities and colleges in Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_and_colleges_in_Manila) and [Division of City Schools–Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_City_Schools%E2%80%93Manila?action=edit\&redlink=1)



The campus of the [Pamantasan ng Lungsod ng Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamantasan_ng_Lungsod_ng_Maynila) and [Baluarte de San Diego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baluarte_de_San_Diego) in Intramuros



[De La Salle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_La_Salle_University) is a [Lasallian educational institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lasallian_educational_institutions) established in 1911.

Manila has been a center of education since the colonial period.[^cite_note-Ricklefs-2010-422] The city has several Philippine universities and colleges, some of which are the county's oldest. The city's [University Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Belt) has a high concentration of colleges and universities, which are a short walking distance of each other. The University Belt is at the boundaries between [San Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel,_Manila), [Quiapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiapo,_Manila), and [Sampaloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampaloc,_Manila) districts, while other clusters colleges lie along the southern bank of the Pasig River – mostly in Intramuros and Ermita districts; and at the southernmost part of [Malate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malate,_Manila) near the city limits.

The historic district Intramuros once housed the [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas) (1611), [Colegio de San Juan de Letran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colegio_de_San_Juan_de_Letran) (1620), and [Ateneo de Manila University](330339.md) (1859).[^cite_note-Ricklefs-2010-422][^cite_note-PACUOrg-MemberSchools-423] Only Colegio de San Juan de Letran remains at Intramuros; the University of Santo Tomas transferred to a new campus at Sampaloc in 1927 and Ateneo de Manila University relocated to Loyola Heights, Quezon City, in 1952. In the 20th century, new non-sectarian schools were built; [Mapúa University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Map%C3%BAa_University) (1925), [Lyceum of the Philippines University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_of_the_Philippines_University) (1952), and [Pamantasan ng Lungsod ng Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamantasan_ng_Lungsod_ng_Maynila) (1965) – which is owned and operated by the Manila city government.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425] The four schools in the district formed the [Intramuros Consortium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros_Consortium).

Other notable universities in Manila include [National University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_\(Philippines\)) (1900), [San Beda University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Beda_University) (1901), the only [Benedictine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedictine) university in Asia, [De La Salle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_La_Salle_University) (1911), the largest of all [De La Salle University System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_La_Salle_University_System) of schools, [St. Paul University Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul_University_Manila) (1912), one of the seven campuses comprising the [St. Paul University System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Paul_University_System) of schools, [Far Eastern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Eastern_University) (1928), and [Adamson University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamson_University) (1939).

The [University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines) (1908), the country's main state university, was established in Ermita, Manila. It moved its central administrative offices from Manila to Diliman in 1949 and eventually made the original campus the [University of the Philippines Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines_Manila), the oldest of the constituent universities of the University of the Philippines System, and the center of health-sciences education in the country.[^cite_note-426] Manila is also the site of the main campus of the [Polytechnic University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnic_University_of_the_Philippines_Santa_Mesa), the largest university in the country in terms of student population.[^cite_note-427]

The city's three-tier public education system, the [Division of the City Schools of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_City_Schools%E2%80%93Manila?action=edit\&redlink=1), is a branch of the [Department of Education](5464548.md). The division oversees 71 public elementary schools and 32 public high schools, all located within the city's territory, except for Rafael Palma Elementary School, which is situated in Barangay La Paz, [Makati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati), near the border with Manila.[^cite_note-428] The city also contains [Manila Science High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Science_High_School), a pilot science high school.[^cite_note-\:1-429]

## International relations

Manila hosts the foreign embassies of the [United States](10576426.md)[^cite_note-462] and Vietnam.[^cite_note-463] Honorary consulates of Belize, Burkina Faso, Jordan, Nepal, Poland, Iceland, Paraguay, Thailand, and Tunisia are based in the city.[^cite_note-DFAGovPH-ForeignConsulates-464]

[^cite_note-64]: &#x20;"In 1637 the military force maintained in the islands consisted of one thousand seven hundred and two Spaniards and one hundred and forty Indians." \~*Memorial de D. Juan Grau y Monfalcon, Procurador General de las Islas Filipinas, Docs. Inéditos del Archivo de Indias, vi, p. 425.* "In 1787 the garrison at Manila consisted of one regiment of Mexicans comprising one thousand three hundred men, two artillery companies of eighty men each, three cavalry companies of fifty men each." *La Pérouse, ii, p. 368.*

[^cite_note-65]: &#x20;(Page 10) Pérez, Marilola (2015). [*Cavite Chabacano Philippine Creole Spanish: Description and Typology*](https://web.archive.org/web/20210114232555/https://escholarship.org/content/qt6xj6f1jt/qt6xj6f1jt_noSplash_fd187448d1120e8904337fe47b42df2a.pdf) (PDF) (PhD). University of California, Berkeley. Archived from [the original](https://escholarship.org/uc/item/6xj6f1jt) on January 14, 2021. The galleon activities also attracted a great number of Mexican men that arrived from the Mexican Pacific coast as ships' crewmembers (Grant 2009: 230). Mexicans were administrators, priests and soldiers (guachinangos or hombres de pueblo) (Bernal 1964: 188) many though, integrated into the peasant society, even becoming tulisanes "bandits" who in the late 18th century "infested" Cavite and led peasant revolts (Medina 2002: 66). Meanwhile, in the Spanish garrisons, Spanish was used among administrators and priests. Nonetheless, there is not enough historical information on the social role of these men. In fact some of the few references point to a quick integration into the local society: "los hombres del pueblo, los soldados y marinos, anónimos, olvidados, absorbidos en su totalidad por la población Filipina." (Bernal 1964: 188). In addition to the Manila-Acapulco galleon, a complex commercial maritime system circulated European and Asian commodities including slaves. During the 17th century, Portuguese vessels traded with the ports of Manila and Cavite, even after the prohibition of 1644 (Seijas 2008: 21). Crucially, the commercial activities included the smuggling and trade of slaves: "from the Moluccas, and Malacca, and India... with the monsoon winds" carrying "clove spice, cinnamon, and pepper and black slaves, and Kafir \[slaves]" (Antonio de Morga cf Seijas 2008: 21)." Though there is no data on the numbers of slaves in Cavite, the numbers in Manila suggest a significant fraction of the population had been brought in as slaves by the Portuguese vessels. By 1621, slaves in Manila numbered 1,970 out of a population of 6,110. This influx of slaves continued until late in the 17th century; according to contemporary cargo records in 1690, 200 slaves departed from Malacca to Manila (Seijas 2008: 21). Different ethnicities were favored for different labor; Africans were brought to work on the agricultural production, and skilled slaves from India served as caulkers and carpenters.&#x20;

[^cite_note-66]: &#x20;["Jesuits In The Philippines (1581-1768)" Page 132](https://archive.org/stream/in.ernet.dli.2015.530346/2015.530346.jesuits-in_djvu.txt) "In 1591 there arrived in Manila a secular priest named Juan Fernandez de Leon. He had led a hermit's life in Mexico and planned to continue it in the Philippines. For this purpose he built himself a retreat near a wayside shrine just outside the city walls which was dedicated to Our Lady of Guidance, Nuestra Sehora de Guia. His hermitage later gave its name to the entire district, which is called Ermita to this day."

[^cite_note-238]: &#x20;Go, Matthew C. (January 15, 2018). ["An Admixture Approach to Trihybrid Ancestry Variation in the Philippines with Implications for Forensic Anthropology"](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/329903258). *Human Biology*. **232** (3): 178. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.13110/humanbiology.90.3.01](https://doi.org/10.13110%2Fhumanbiology.90.3.01). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [33947174](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33947174). Retrieved September 11, 2020. Filipinos appear considerably admixed with respect to the other Asian population samples, carrying on average less Asian ancestry (71%) than our Korean (99%), Japanese (96%), Thai (93%), and Vietnamese (84%) reference samples. We also revealed substructure in our Filipino sample, showing that the patterns of ancestry vary within the Philippines—that is, between the four differently sourced Filipino samples. Mean estimates of Asian (76%) and European (7%) ancestry are greatest for the cemetery sample of forensic significance from Manila.

[^cite_note-PhilippineAnthropologyStudy-239]: &#x20;Go MC, Jones AR, Algee-Hewitt B, Dudzik B, Hughes C (2019). ["Classification Trends among Contemporary Filipino Crania Using Fordisc 3.1"](https://www.academia.edu/38744342). *Human Biology*. **2** (4). University of Florida Press: 293–303. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.5744/fa.2019.1005](https://doi.org/10.5744%2Ffa.2019.1005). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [159266278](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:159266278). Retrieved September 13, 2020. \[Page 1] ABSTRACT: Filipinos represent a significant contemporary demographic group globally, yet they are underrepresented in the forensic anthropological literature. Given the complex population history of the Philippines, it is important to ensure that traditional methods for assessing the biological profile are appropriate when applied to these peoples. Here we analyze the classification trends of a modern Filipino sample (n = 110) when using the Fordisc 3.1 (FD3) software. We hypothesize that Filipinos represent an admixed population drawn largely from Asian and marginally from European parental gene pools, such that FD3 will classify these individuals morphometrically into reference samples that reflect a range of European admixture, in quantities from small to large. Our results show the greatest classification into Asian reference groups (72.7%), followed by Hispanic (12.7%), Indigenous American (7.3%), African (4.5%), and European (2.7%) groups included in FD3. This general pattern did not change between males and females. Moreover, replacing the raw craniometric values with their shape variables did not significantly alter the trends already observed. These classification trends for Filipino crania provide useful information for casework interpretation in forensic laboratory practice. Our findings can help biological anthropologists to better understand the evolutionary, population historical, and statistical reasons for FD3-generated classifications. The results of our study indicate that ancestry estimation in forensic anthropology would benefit from population-focused research that gives consideration to histories of colonialism and periods of admixture.
